<a id="eq:random-extension"></a>
<a id="eq:extension-gate"></a>
For $h\in\{1,2\}$, let $m,m+h\in\mathcal I_N$, and let $C$ be a nonsingular binary matrix of size $m$. A *random extension of width $h$* is $$\label{eq:random-extension}
 B=\begin{pmatrix}C&V\\ V^{\mathsf T}&D\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad V=(v_1\ \cdots\ v_h).$$ The $mh$ entries of $V$ and the $h(h+1)/2$ entries $D_{ab}$, $1\le a\le b\le h$, are mutually independent uniform bits; the lower triangle of $D$ is determined by symmetry. If $C$ is random, all these new bits are also independent of $C$. Thus, conditional on the entire matrix $C$, the columns $v_1,\ldots,v_h$ are independent uniform elements of $\{0,1\}^m$, independent of all upper-triangular entries of $D$. Writing $\Omega$ for this extension’s sample space, define the gate events by $$\label{eq:extension-gate}
 \mathcal G_1=\Omega,\qquad
 \mathcal G_2=\{v_1\in\mathcal S(C),\ v_2\in\mathcal S(C)\}.$$ Conditional independence of the two columns gives <a id="eq:gate-probability"></a>$$\label{eq:gate-probability}
 \Pr(\mathcal G_2\mid C)=\left(\frac{|\mathcal S(C)|}{2^m}\right)^2.$$ This identity lets the admissible-column estimate pay for the potential bound on extensions of width two. Lemma <a href="#lem:gated-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gated-deletion">26</a> shows that every nonsingular matrix has a backward move whose forward extension satisfies its gate.

<a id="stmt-prop-potential-step"></a>
<div id="prop:potential-step" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (Potential step). *For every fixed $A>0$, the following holds typically for nonsingular binary matrices $C$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$. For each $h\in\{1,2\}$ with $m+h\in\mathcal I_N$, the random extension in Equation <a href="#eq:random-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-extension">[eq:random-extension]</a> satisfies <a id="eq:potential-step"></a>$$\label{eq:potential-step}
 \mathbb E\!\left[
   \mathbf 1_{\{\det B\ne0\}}\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G_h}\,2^{N\Psi(B)}
   \,\middle|\, C\right]
 \le 2^{N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A})}.$$ The typical condition is imposed only on the starting matrix $C$.*

</div>

Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a> will be proved in Section <a href="#sec:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:potential-step">10</a>. We first establish the algebraic facts that relate an extension to quotients of $G_C$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The common quotient and its layers

<a id="eq:successive-quotients"></a>
<a id="eq:common-quotient"></a>
Fix a realization of Equation <a href="#eq:random-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-extension">[eq:random-extension]</a> for which $B$ is nonsingular. Set $$\label{eq:successive-quotients}
 H_0=G_C,\qquad
 H_j=\mathbb Z^m/(C\mathbb Z^m+\mathbb Zv_1+\cdots+\mathbb Zv_j)
     =H_{j-1}/\langle\overline v_j\rangle
       \quad(1\le j\le h),$$ where $\overline v_j$ is the image of $v_j$ in $H_{j-1}$. These finite groups depend only on $C$ and the indicated columns. They are defined whenever $C$ is nonsingular, even if the destination matrix $B$ is singular. Nonsingularity of $B$ is needed only when we refer to its discriminant group. The last one is also a quotient of $G_B$: $$\label{eq:common-quotient}
 H_h\cong G_B/\langle\overline e_{m+1},\ldots,
                         \overline e_{m+h}\rangle,$$ where the bars on the right denote classes of standard basis vectors in $G_B$. Indeed, the right side is $$\mathbb Z^{m+h}/\bigl(B\mathbb Z^{m+h}+(0\oplus\mathbb Z^h)\bigr).$$ Projection onto the first $m$ coordinates maps its relations to $C\mathbb Z^m+V\mathbb Z^h$. Conversely, if a vector in $\mathbb Z^{m+h}$ projects into $C\mathbb Z^m+V\mathbb Z^h$, subtracting a suitable vector of $B\mathbb Z^{m+h}$ leaves a vector in $0\oplus\mathbb Z^h$. Projection therefore induces exactly the isomorphism in Equation <a href="#eq:common-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-quotient">[eq:common-quotient]</a>.

For a finite abelian group $G$, a prime $p$, and an integer $t\ge0$, define <a id="eq:layer-prefix"></a>$$\label{eq:layer-prefix}
 F_G(p,t)=\sum_{e=1}^{t}k_G(p,e)
         =\sum_i\min\{b_{p,i},t\}
         =\log_p|G/p^tG|.$$ The empty sum is zero. The two equalities follow directly from the cyclic decomposition of the $p$-primary part; multiplication by $p^t$ is an automorphism on every other primary part. For a nonsingular binary matrix $M$, write $F_M(p,t)=F_{G_M}(p,t)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-layer-quotient"></a>
<div id="lem:layer-quotient" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:quotient-prefix"></a>
<a id="eq:layer-interlacing"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Layers under a one-element quotient). *Let $G$ be a finite abelian group, let $v\in G$, and put $H=G/\langle v\rangle$. Fix a prime $p$, and define $u_t$ by $$p^{u_t}=|\langle v+p^tG\rangle|\quad\hbox{in }G/p^tG
 \qquad(t\ge0).$$ Then $u_0=0$, $u_t-u_{t-1}\in\{0,1\}$ for $t\ge1$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 F_H(p,t)&=F_G(p,t)-u_t,\label{eq:quotient-prefix}\\
 k_H(p,e)&=k_G(p,e)-(u_e-u_{e-1})\in
           \{k_G(p,e),k_G(p,e)-1\}.\label{eq:layer-interlacing}
\end{aligned}$$ Call a positive layer $(p,e)$ of $G$ *retained* if $k_H(p,e)=k_G(p,e)$. On every maximal interval of consecutive layers where $k_G(p,e)$ has a fixed positive value, the retained layers form an initial interval, possibly empty. We call such an interval of constant height a *plateau*.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-layer-quotient"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-layer-quotient</summary>

*Proof.* Quotienting by $p^t$ and by $v$ commutes: $$H/p^tH\cong (G/p^tG)/\langle v+p^tG\rangle.$$ Taking orders proves Equation <a href="#eq:quotient-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient-prefix">[eq:quotient-prefix]</a>.

<a id="eq:quotient-order-exponent"></a>
Write $G_p=\bigoplus_i\mathbb Z/p^{b_i}\mathbb Z$. For the $i$-th coordinate of the $p$-primary component of $v$, let $r_i$ be its $p$-adic valuation capped at $b_i$; in particular $r_i=b_i$ for a zero coordinate. The order of that coordinate in $G_p/p^tG_p$ is $p^{(\min\{b_i,t\}-r_i)_+}$, where $x_+=\max\{x,0\}$. The order of an element in a direct sum of $p$-groups is the largest of its coordinate orders, so $$\label{eq:quotient-order-exponent}
 u_t=\max_i(\min\{b_i,t\}-r_i)_+,$$ with maximum zero when $G_p$ is trivial. Each function under this maximum is nondecreasing in $t$ and increases by at most one at a step. The same is therefore true of $u_t$; since it is integer valued, its increments belong to $\{0,1\}$. Subtracting Equation <a href="#eq:quotient-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient-prefix">[eq:quotient-prefix]</a> at $t=e-1$ from the equation at $t=e$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:layer-interlacing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-interlacing">[eq:layer-interlacing]</a>.

On a plateau of height $k$, the heights in $H$ are either $k$ or $k-1$. They are nonincreasing as $e$ increases, by their definition from invariant factors. Thus a height $k-1$ cannot be followed on the plateau by height $k$. The retained layers are exactly those of height $k$, proving the prefix assertion. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:layers">

<figcaption>Layers for $G_{p} = ℤ/p^{5}ℤ ⊕ ℤ/p^{3}ℤ ⊕ ℤ/pℤ$ and its quotient by $v = (p^{2},p,1)$. The crossed boxes are the layer drops. Here $(u_{1},…,u_{5}) = (1,1,2,2,3)$, so the retained layers are $e = 2, 4$. On each plateau $e = 2, 3$ and $e = 4, 5$, retention is a prefix. The row lengths on the right are the heights of the quotient.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-layers"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:layers`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.62cm,y=-0.62cm,
                    every node/.style={font=\small},
                    line width=0.4pt]
 \node at (1.5,-0.65) {\(G_p\)};
 \node[anchor=east] at (-0.25,-0.65) {\(e\)};
 \foreach \e/\k in {1/3,2/2,3/2,4/1,5/1} {
  \node[anchor=east] at (-0.25,{\e-0.5}) {\(\e\)};
  \foreach \i in {1,...,\k} {
   \draw ({\i-1},{\e-1}) rectangle (\i,\e);
  }
 }
 \foreach \e/\i in {1/3,3/2,5/1} {
  \draw[fill=black!12] ({\i-1},{\e-1}) rectangle (\i,\e);
  \node at ({\i-0.5},{\e-0.5}) {\(\times\)};
 }
 \node[anchor=west] at (3.25,1.5) {retained};
 \node[anchor=west] at (3.25,3.5) {retained};
 \draw[->] (6.5,2.5) -- (8.7,2.5);
 \node at (7.6,1.85) {quotient by \(v\)};
 \node at (11.5,-0.65) {\(G_p/\langle v\rangle\)};
 \foreach \e/\k in {1/2,2/2,3/1,4/1} {
  \node[anchor=east] at (10.25,{\e-0.5}) {\(\e\)};
  \foreach \i in {1,...,\k} {
   \draw ({\i+9.5},{\e-1}) rectangle ({\i+10.5},\e);
  }
 }
 \node[anchor=east] at (10.25,4.5) {\(5\)};
 \node[anchor=west] at (10.5,4.5) {height \(0\)};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

  - Row 1: 3 square boxes, with an "×" inside the 3rd box.
  - Row 2: 2 square boxes; labeled "retained" to the right of the row.
  - Row 3: 2 square boxes, with an "×" inside the 2nd box.
  - Row 4: 1 square box; labeled "retained" to the right of the row.
  - Row 5: 1 square box, with an "×" inside the box.
- Center: horizontal arrow pointing right labeled "quotient by v".

  - Row 1: 2 square boxes.

Applied successively to Equation <a href="#eq:successive-quotients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:successive-quotients">[eq:successive-quotients]</a>, this lemma gives a drop of zero or one at each layer in each quotient. The same statement applies to the $h$ successive quotients of $G_B$ by the standard basis classes in Equation <a href="#eq:common-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-quotient">[eq:common-quotient]</a>. Consequently, for every $(p,e)$, <a id="eq:common-layer-interlacing"></a>$$\label{eq:common-layer-interlacing}
 k_C(p,e)-h\le k_{H_h}(p,e)\le k_C(p,e),\qquad
 k_B(p,e)-h\le k_{H_h}(p,e)\le k_B(p,e).$$ In particular, for $h=1$ the common height is either $k_C(p,e)$ or $k_C(p,e)-1$, while $k_B(p,e)$ is either the common height or one more. This also shows that a layer absent from $G_C$ has height at most $h$ in $G_B$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The lattice forced by retention

The next lemma extracts from a set of retained layers a necessary divisibility condition on the column, with an exact formula for its index. The condition lies in an intermediate lattice between $C\mathbb Z^m$ and $\mathbb Z^m$, including when one preceding column has already been quotiented out.

<a id="stmt-lem-retention-lattice"></a>
<div id="lem:retention-lattice" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:retention-lattice"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Retention lattice). *Let $C$ be a nonsingular binary matrix of size $m$, and let $\pi:\mathbb Z^m\twoheadrightarrow H$ be a quotient map to a finite abelian group such that $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\ker\pi$. Let $E$ be any set of positive layers of $H$. There is a lattice $K_E$, depending only on $\pi,H,E$, with $$\label{eq:retention-lattice}
 C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K_E\subseteq\mathbb Z^m,\qquad
 [\mathbb Z^m:K_E]=N^{Nq(E)},\qquad
 q(E)=\sum_{(p,e)\in E} k_H(p,e)\nu_p,$$ such that the following implication holds for every $v\in\mathbb Z^m$: if every layer in $E$ is retained in $H\longrightarrow H/\langle\pi(v)\rangle$, then $v\in K_E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-retention-lattice"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-retention-lattice</summary>

*Proof.* For each prime choose, once for the given group $H$, cyclic coordinates $$H_p=\bigoplus_i\mathbb Z/p^{b_i}\mathbb Z.$$ Put $$a_{p,i}=\#\{e:(p,e)\in E,\ e\le b_i\},\qquad
 T_E=\bigoplus_{p,i}p^{a_{p,i}}(\mathbb Z/p^{b_i}\mathbb Z)\subseteq H,
 \qquad K_E=\pi^{-1}(T_E).$$ Here $0\le a_{p,i}\le b_i$, and the subgroup in the $(p,i)$ coordinate consists of the classes divisible by $p^{a_{p,i}}$. The lattice $K_E$ contains $C\mathbb Z^m$. Its index is $$[\mathbb Z^m:K_E]=[H:T_E]
 =\prod_{p,i}p^{a_{p,i}}
 =\prod_{(p,e)\in E}p^{k_H(p,e)}
 =N^{Nq(E)},$$ where the penultimate equality counts a selected layer once for each cyclic factor reaching it. This proves all assertions about the lattice and its index.

It remains to prove the implication. Fix $p,i$, and let $r_i$ be the capped valuation of the $i$-th coordinate of $\pi(v)$, as in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-quotient">28</a>. Equation <a href="#eq:quotient-order-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient-order-exponent">[eq:quotient-order-exponent]</a> at $t=b_i$ gives $u_{b_i}\ge b_i-r_i$. All the first $b_i$ layers are positive, and Equation <a href="#eq:layer-interlacing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-interlacing">[eq:layer-interlacing]</a> shows that the number among them that are retained is $$\sum_{e=1}^{b_i}\bigl(1-(u_e-u_{e-1})\bigr)=b_i-u_{b_i}.$$ If every layer in $E$ is retained, then $$a_{p,i}\le b_i-u_{b_i}\le r_i.$$ Thus the coordinate of $\pi(v)$ is divisible by $p^{a_{p,i}}$. This holds in every primary coordinate, so $\pi(v)\in T_E$ and $v\in K_E$, as required. ◻

</details>

For the group in Figure <a href="#fig:layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:layers">1</a>, choosing $E=\{(p,2),(p,4)\}$ gives divisibility exponents $(a_{p,1},a_{p,2},a_{p,3})=(2,1,0)$. The two selected layers have total weight $2\nu_p$, while $q(E)=3\nu_p$ and $[\mathbb Z^m:K_E]=p^3$.

For a quotient in Equation <a href="#eq:successive-quotients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:successive-quotients">[eq:successive-quotients]</a>, the map $\pi$ is the natural map from $\mathbb Z^m$ onto $H_{j-1}$. Conditional on $C$ and the preceding columns $v_1,\ldots,v_{j-1}$, the next column remains uniform on the cube.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A symmetric kernel inequality

<a id="eq:total-quotient-loss"></a>
The common quotient also controls the destination layers more strongly than interlacing alone. At a fixed prime $p$, let $u_{j,t}$ denote the exponent from Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-quotient">28</a> for $H_{j-1}\to H_j$, and set $$\label{eq:total-quotient-loss}
 U_t=\sum_{j=1}^h u_{j,t}
    =F_C(p,t)-F_{H_h}(p,t).$$ The equality follows by summing Equation <a href="#eq:quotient-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient-prefix">[eq:quotient-prefix]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-layer-inequality"></a>
<div id="lem:layer-inequality" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:layer-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Symmetric kernel inequality). *For a nonsingular extension $B$ of a nonsingular $C$ of width $h\in\{1,2\}$, at every prime $p$ and every integer $t\ge0$, $$\label{eq:layer-inequality}
 F_B(p,t)\le F_C(p,t)+ht-2U_t.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-layer-inequality"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-layer-inequality</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate for $t=0$. Fix $t\ge1$, put $R_t=\mathbb Z/p^t\mathbb Z$, and reduce the blocks of $B$ modulo $p^t$, writing them as $C_t,V_t,D_t$. Let $$X=R_t^m,\qquad Y=R_t^h,\qquad
 L=\ker(C_t:X\to X),\qquad J=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(C_t:X\to X).$$ The presentation of the cokernel gives $$X/J\cong \mathbb Z^m/(C\mathbb Z^m+p^t\mathbb Z^m)\cong G_C/p^tG_C.$$ Since $C_t$ is an endomorphism of the finite group $X$, its kernel and cokernel have the same order. Hence <a id="eq:kernel-prefix-order"></a>$$\label{eq:kernel-prefix-order}
 |L|=|X/J|=p^{F_C(p,t)}.$$

<a id="eq:first-block-kernel"></a>
Define $$\alpha:Y\longrightarrow X/J,\qquad \alpha(y)=V_ty+J.$$ The image is the subgroup generated by the column classes in $G_C/p^tG_C$. Quotienting by that subgroup gives $H_h/p^tH_h$, so Equation <a href="#eq:total-quotient-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-quotient-loss">[eq:total-quotient-loss]</a> gives $$|\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\alpha|=p^{U_t},\qquad |\ker\alpha|=p^{ht-U_t}.$$ For a kernel vector $(x,y)\in X\oplus Y$ of $B$ modulo $p^t$, the first block equation is $$\label{eq:first-block-kernel}
 C_tx+V_ty=0.$$ It requires $y\in\ker\alpha$. For each such $y$, its solutions in $x$ form a coset $x_0+L$.

<a id="eq:annihilator-order"></a>
Consider the map $$\gamma:L\longrightarrow Y,\qquad \gamma(z)=V_t^{\mathsf T}z.$$ We claim that $|\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\gamma|=p^{U_t}$. Equip $X$ and $Y$ with their standard character pairings, for example $$\langle x,z\rangle_X
   =\exp\!\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^t}x^{\mathsf T}z\right).$$ These pairings are perfect: a nonzero coordinate is detected by pairing with its standard basis vector. For a subgroup $M$ of either module $A$, write $M^\perp$ for its annihilator. Character orthogonality gives $$\label{eq:annihilator-order}
 |M|\,|M^\perp|
 =\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{z\in M}\langle a,z\rangle_A
 =|A|.$$ For the first equality, the sum over $M$ is $|M|$ on its annihilator and zero elsewhere. For the second, reverse the sums: by perfectness the sum over $A$ vanishes for every $z\ne0$, and the term $z=0$ equals $|A|$.

Symmetry of $C_t$ gives $J\subseteq L^\perp$, since $\langle C_tx,z\rangle_X=\langle x,C_tz\rangle_X=1$ for $z\in L$. Both groups have order $|X|/|L|$, the former by the kernel-image formula and the latter by Equation <a href="#eq:annihilator-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:annihilator-order">[eq:annihilator-order]</a>. Therefore $L^\perp=J$. It follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 (\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\gamma)^\perp
  &=\{y\in Y:\langle y,V_t^{\mathsf T}z\rangle_Y=1
                    \text{ for every }z\in L\}\\
  &=\{y\in Y:\langle V_ty,z\rangle_X=1
                    \text{ for every }z\in L\}\\
  &=\{y\in Y:V_ty\in J\}=\ker\alpha.
\end{aligned}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:annihilator-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:annihilator-order">[eq:annihilator-order]</a> in $Y$ now gives $$|\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\gamma|=\frac{|Y|}{|\ker\alpha|}=p^{U_t},
 \qquad
 |\ker\gamma|=\frac{|L|}{|\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\gamma|}
              =p^{F_C(p,t)-U_t}.$$

For a fixed $y\in\ker\alpha$ and $x=x_0+z$ from Equation <a href="#eq:first-block-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-block-kernel">[eq:first-block-kernel]</a>, the second block equation is $$\gamma(z)=-V_t^{\mathsf T}x_0-D_ty.$$ It has either no solutions or one coset of $\ker\gamma$. There are at most $p^{ht-U_t}$ choices for $y$, and for each at most $p^{F_C(p,t)-U_t}$ choices for $z$. Consequently $$|\ker(B\bmod p^t)|\le p^{F_C(p,t)+ht-2U_t}.$$ As for $C_t$, the kernel of the square endomorphism $B\bmod p^t$ has the same order as its cokernel. That cokernel is $G_B/p^tG_B$, of order $p^{F_B(p,t)}$. Taking logarithms proves Equation <a href="#eq:layer-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-inequality">[eq:layer-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

Lemma <a href="#lem:retention-lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:retention-lattice">29</a> identifies the intermediate lattices that arise from retaining layers, while Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-inequality">30</a> uses symmetry to relate those retentions to the destination group. Section <a href="#sec:cube" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cube">8</a> supplies a uniform bound on cube intersections with every lattice of the form in Equation <a href="#eq:retention-lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retention-lattice">[eq:retention-lattice]</a>.

<a id="sec:cube"></a>
### Simultaneous concentration in lattice quotients

Retaining prescribed layers forces a new bit column into an intermediate lattice. We now bound the number of bit columns in every coset of every such lattice, on one typical event for the starting matrix.

Let $B$ be a nonsingular binary matrix of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, and let $$B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m.$$ For $D\subset[m]$, put $$Q_D=\{z\in\{0,1\}^m:z_j=0\text{ for }j\notin D\},\qquad
 M_K(D)=\max_{a\in\mathbb Z^m}|Q_D\cap(a+K)|,
 \qquad M_K=M_K([m]).$$ In particular, $M_K(D')\le M_K(D)$ whenever $D'\subset D$. The lattice $K$ has full rank, so $\det K=[\mathbb Z^m:K]$. Define $$q=q_K=\frac{\log[\mathbb Z^m:K]}{N\log N},
 \qquad\text{so that}\qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K]=N^{qN}.$$ The quotient $\mathbb Z^m/K$ is a quotient of $G_B$, so Equation <a href="#eq:group-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:group-order">[eq:group-order]</a> gives $0\le q\le1/2$.

<a id="eq:cube-profile"></a>
For $0\le d\le1$, write $f=1-d$ and define $$\label{eq:cube-profile}
 \begin{split}
 c(d/2)&=1-fz(d/f),\\
 z(u)&=
 \begin{cases}
 (1+\sqrt{2u-u^2})^{-1},&0\le u\le1,\\
 1/2,&u\ge1.
 \end{cases}
 \end{split}$$ At $f=0$, the product $fz(d/f)$ is defined to be zero. Thus $c:[0,1/2]\to[0,1]$, with $c(0)=0$ and $c(1/2)=1$.

<a id="stmt-prop-cube-concentration"></a>
<div id="prop:cube-concentration" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:cube-concentration"></a>
**Proposition 31** (Cube concentration). *For every fixed $A>0$, typically for nonsingular binary matrices $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, one has simultaneously for every lattice $B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m$ $$\label{eq:cube-concentration}
 M_K\le 2^{N(1-c(q_K)+\ell^{-A})}.$$ The typical event may depend on $A$, but it does not depend on $K$. Thus the choice of $K$ may depend arbitrarily on $B$ and on any other data.*

</div>

The function $c$ is nondecreasing and concave. To see this, put $r(u)=\sqrt{2u-u^2}$ for $0<u<1$. Then $$r'(u)=\frac{1-u}{r(u)},\qquad r''(u)=-\frac1{r(u)^3}.$$ Consequently $z=(1+r)^{-1}$ is nonincreasing and convex on $[0,1]$; more explicitly, $$z''(u)=\frac{1}{r(u)^3(1+r(u))^2}
       +\frac{2(1-u)^2}{r(u)^2(1+r(u))^3}>0.$$ Its left derivative at $1$ is zero, so its constant extension remains convex and nonincreasing. The perspective $fz(d/f)$ is convex for $f>0$: this follows by applying convexity of $z$ with weights proportional to the two values of $f$. Its continuous extension to the endpoint $f=0$ is also convex. Restriction to $f=1-d$ proves concavity of $c$. As $d$ increases, both $f$ and $z(d/f)$ are nonnegative and nonincreasing, which proves monotonicity.

<a id="eq:cube-holder"></a>
We will use the uniform bound $$\label{eq:cube-holder}
 |c(q)-c(q')|\le C_{\mathrm{pr}}|q-q'|^{1/2}
 \qquad(0\le q,q'\le1/2)$$ for an absolute constant $C_{\mathrm{pr}}$. Indeed, for $u,v\in[0,1]$, $$|z(u)-z(v)|\le |r(u)-r(v)|
 \le \sqrt{2|u-v|}.$$ On $0\le d\le1/2$, the map $d\mapsto d/(1-d)$ is $4$-Lipschitz, so the product in Equation <a href="#eq:cube-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-profile">[eq:cube-profile]</a> is Hölder of exponent $1/2$ there. On $1/2\le d\le1$ it is $(1-d)/2$. Combining the two bounds at $d=1/2$, and then using $d=2q$, gives Equation <a href="#eq:cube-holder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-holder">[eq:cube-holder]</a>.

We first count bases of low-height subspaces, and then use that count to price the witnesses that will arise from a large cube intersection.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Low-height bases and witness pricing

<a id="stmt-lem-exact-basis-count"></a>
<div id="lem:exact-basis-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Exact-basis count). *Fix $C,C'>0$. Uniformly for $m\in\mathcal I_N$ and $0\le j\le m$, the number of ordered tuples $$(y,y_1,\ldots,y_j)\in(\{0,1\}^m)^{j+1}$$ for which $y_1-y,\ldots,y_j-y$ are independent and their span $W$ satisfies $H(W)\le\exp(N\ell^C)$ is at most $$2^{j^2+o(N^2\ell^{-C'})}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exact-basis-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exact-basis-count</summary>

*Proof.* The case $j=0$ is immediate. For $j\ge1$, encode the center $y$ and a choice of $j$ pivot coordinates on which the differences remain independent. These choices cost $O(N\log N)$ bits. At the pivots, each difference entry has two possible values once $y$ is known, so all pivot entries cost $j^2$ bits.

Add the other coordinates in a fixed order. Suppose the current projected span is $V\subset\mathbb R^h$, and put $L_V=V\cap\mathbb Z^h$. The next projected span is the graph of a rational functional $x\mapsto\langle v,x\rangle$ on $V$, represented by $v\in V$. Put $$a=\min\{b\in\mathbb Z_{>0}:bv\in L_V^*\}.$$ The image of $L_V$ under this functional modulo $\mathbb Z$ has order $a$. Hence the projection of the integer lattice in the graph has index $a$ in $L_V$. The volume factor of the graph map on $V$ is $\sqrt{1+\|v\|^2}$, and therefore <a id="eq:cube-height-ratio"></a>$$\label{eq:cube-height-ratio}
 \frac{H(\operatorname{graph}(v))}{H(V)}
       =a\sqrt{1+\|v\|^2}.$$ All these ratios are at least one. The initial projected space is $\mathbb R^j$, of height one, and the final one is $W$. Thus every intermediate height is at most $\exp(N\ell^C)$, and a ratio exceeding $N^{1/10}$ occurs at most $O(N\ell^C/\log N)$ times. Mark those coordinates and encode their $j$ difference entries directly. Their total cost is $O(N^2\ell^C/\log N)$ bits.

At any other coordinate, $$a\le N^{1/10},\qquad \|av\|\le N^{1/10}.$$ We count possible $av$ in $\Lambda=L_V^*$. Lemma <a href="#lem:normal-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:normal-height">11</a>, applied to $V\subset\mathbb R^h$, gives $\det M\ge H(V)^{-1}$ for every sublattice $M\subset\Lambda$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:det-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:det-minimizer">14</a> to $\Lambda$ with $T=N^{-1/10}$, choosing a minimizing sublattice as a function of the known $V$. If its rank is $r_0$, comparison with the zero lattice and the preceding determinant bound give $$H(V)^{-1}\le T^{r_0},\qquad
 r_0\le \frac{10N\ell^C}{\log N}.$$ Every sublattice of the quotient has determinant at least $T$ to its rank. By Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-count">13</a>, the number of quotient images of vectors of norm at most $N^{1/10}$ is $$2^{O(N^{2/5}\log^2N)}.$$ Here the quotient has rank at most $N$, and the bound also covers rank zero.

Encode $a$, costing $O(\log N)$ bits, and the quotient image of $av$. In a fixed fiber, $v$ varies in an affine space of dimension at most $r_0$. Evaluation on the already known $j$ independent projected differences is injective on $V$, and the evaluations are the $j$ entries of the new difference column. Each entry has two possible values, determined by the corresponding coordinate of $y$. An affine space of dimension $r_0$ meets such a cube in at most $2^{r_0}$ points, by projection onto $r_0$ suitable coordinates. This bounds the fiber cost by $r_0$ bits.

Summing over at most $N$ coordinates, including the pivot, center, and marking costs, bounds the logarithm of the number of tuples by $$j^2+O\!\left(
   \frac{N^2\ell^C}{\log N}+N^{7/5}\log^2N+N\log N
 \right).$$ For fixed $C,C'$, the error is $o(N^2\ell^{-C'})$, uniformly in $j$ and $m$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-witness-pricing"></a>
<div id="prop:witness-pricing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33** (Witness pricing). *Fix $D_0>0$, and put $\eta=\ell^{-D_0}$ and $\rho=\eta^3$. Typically for nonsingular binary matrices $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, the following assertion holds simultaneously for every $B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m$.*

<a id="eq:witness-pricing"></a>
*Let $S_1,\ldots,S_h\subset\{0,1\}^m$ be nonempty subsets, each $\rho$-robust on its affine span, where $h\le3/\eta$. Suppose $S_i-S_i\subset K$, and put $W_i=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(S_i-S_i)$. Suppose also that $W=\sum_i W_i$ is a direct sum. Write $$\dim W_i=t_iN,\qquad t=\sum_i t_i,\qquad |S_i|=2^{\alpha_iN}.$$ If $D\subset[m]$ has $|D|=xN$, $x\le t$, and $M_K(D)\le2^{\eta N}$, then $$\label{eq:witness-pricing}
 xt-\frac{x^2}{2}
 \le \sum_i t_i(t_i-\alpha_i)+\eta t+\eta^3.$$*

</div>

For a fixed nonsingular matrix $B$, a *witness* consists of data $K,(S_i)_{i=1}^h,D$ satisfying the stated assumptions on the lattice, sets, their difference spans, and $D$. It is *violating* if Equation <a href="#eq:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-pricing">[eq:witness-pricing]</a> fails. The two sides of that equation have a counting interpretation. In a symmetric block on $tN$ coordinates, the number of independent entries with at least one endpoint in a specified set of $xN$ coordinates is $(xt-x^2/2)N^2+O(N)$. Restricting each row on those coordinates to at most $2^{\eta N}$ choices saves this many bits, less $\eta tN^2$. For the $i$-th set $S_i$, describing an ordered basis costs $t_i^2N^2$ bits to leading order, while robustness supplies $\alpha_i t_iN^2$ bits of basis multiplicity. The term $t_i(t_i-\alpha_i)$ is the remaining cost. A violation would make the row saving exceed the total witness cost by $\eta^3N^2$.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-witness-pricing"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-witness-pricing</summary>

*Proof.* The robust-height bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:robust-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:robust-height">16</a>, with the fixed parameter $\rho=\ell^{-3D_0}$, gives $$H(W_i)\le\exp(O(N\ell)).$$ The integer lattice sum of $W_i\cap\mathbb Z^m$ is contained in $W\cap\mathbb Z^m$. The volume inequality for this sum and saturation give $$H(W)\le\prod_i H(W_i)
       \le\exp(O(N\ell/\eta)).$$ Choose a fixed $D_1>D_0+1$. For sufficiently large $N$, this is at most $\exp(N\ell^{D_1})$. We impose Proposition <a href="#prop:typical-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:typical-ranks">18</a> at this height and at a fixed precision strong enough that its rank error is at most $\eta^5N$. We also impose Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-corank">20</a>. Their intersection is typical. All counting below takes place within this intersection.

<a id="eq:witness-basis-upper"></a>
For a fixed list of dimensions, Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-basis-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-basis-count">32</a> counts centers and ordered bases of differences for the pieces with total logarithmic cost $$\label{eq:witness-basis-upper}
 N^2\sum_i t_i^2+o(N^2\eta^4).$$ Indeed, use that Lemma with a fixed $C'$ larger than $5D_0$ and sum its errors over at most $3/\eta$ pieces. The height exponent there can be chosen fixed and large enough for all the $W_i$.

<a id="eq:witness-basis-lower"></a>
For each individual witness, fix a center in each $S_i$. At every stage of choosing an ordered basis of differences, the affine span of the center and the points already chosen is a proper affine subspace of $\mathop{\mathrm{aff}}S_i$. Robustness bounds the fraction of $S_i$ in that subspace by $2^{-\rho}$. Consequently the number of choices of an ordered basis for this piece is at least $$\bigl((1-2^{-\rho})\,2^{\alpha_iN}\bigr)^{t_iN}.$$ The choices for different pieces form distinct basis lists. As $t\le m/N\le1$, their total logarithm is at least $$\label{eq:witness-basis-lower}
 N^2\sum_i t_i\alpha_i
       -O\bigl(N\log(1/\rho)\bigr)
 =N^2\sum_i t_i\alpha_i-o(N^2\eta^4).$$

Fix basis-list data, so in particular $W$ is known. For a witnessed matrix choose a coordinate set $Y_0$ of size $tN$ containing $D$. The second rank assertion of Proposition <a href="#prop:typical-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:typical-ranks">18</a> shows that the columns on $[m]\setminus Y_0$, projected off $W$, have rank at least $m-tN-\eta^5N$. Choose an independent collection there, and use nonsingularity of $B$ to complete it to a basis of the quotient $\mathbb R^m/W$ using columns of $B$. Let $P$ be the resulting set of $m-tN$ indices and $Y=[m]\setminus P$. Then $$|D\cap P|\le\eta^5N.$$ Encode $D$ and $P$, at cost $O(N)$ bits, and reveal the columns on $P$. If $T_m=m(m+1)/2$ is the total number of independent bits of $B$, these columns cost $$T_m-\binom{tN+1}{2}$$ bits, using symmetry.

The revealed columns and the basis differences are $m$ independent integer vectors: the differences form a basis of $W$, and the revealed columns project to a basis off $W$. Let $L$ be their integer span. It is known from the encoded data, satisfies $L\subset K$, and has full rank. Each generating vector has norm at most $\sqrt N$, so Hadamard’s inequality gives $$|\mathbb Z^m/L|\le N^{m/2}\le N^{N/2}.$$ We next enumerate the possible $K$ containing this known $L$. Let $r_N=\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$. For every prime $p$, $$\dim_{\mathbb F_p}\bigl((\mathbb Z^m/K)/p(\mathbb Z^m/K)\bigr)
 \le\dim_{\mathbb F_p}(G_B/pG_B)
 =\mathop{\mathrm{corank}}_{\mathbb F_p}B\le r_N$$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-corank">20</a>. The minimum number of generators of a finite abelian group is the maximum of these dimensions over its primes, as follows from its invariant factors. Thus $\mathbb Z^m/K$, and also its character dual, can be generated by at most $r_N$ elements.

<a id="eq:witness-lattice-cost"></a>
Put $H=\mathbb Z^m/L$. The annihilator of $K/L$ identifies $(\mathbb Z^m/K)^\vee$ with a subgroup of $H^\vee=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(H,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)$; by finite duality this subgroup determines $K$. Every subgroup with at most $r_N$ generators is generated by some ordered $r_N$-tuple in $H^\vee$, padding with zero elements if necessary. The number of possible $K$ is therefore at most $|H|^{r_N}$. Its logarithm is $$\label{eq:witness-lattice-cost}
 O(N^{8/5}\log N)=o(N^2\eta^4).$$ This candidate list is formed after the basis lists and revealed columns fix $L$, before the remaining matrix block is encoded. It may include lattices incompatible with every completion. The prime-corank condition is used only to ensure that each actual witnessed $K$ appears in the list.

Write $D'=D\cap Y$, $y=|Y|=tN$, and $d'=|D'|$. We have $d'\ge(x-\eta^5)N$ and $M_K(D')\le M_K(D)\le2^{\eta N}$. In the remaining $Y$-block, first encode the symmetric bits on $Y\setminus D'$, at cost $\binom{y-d'+1}{2}$. For a row indexed by $Y\setminus D'$, all entries outside $D'$ are now known. The full row belongs to $K$, since $B$ is symmetric and each column belongs to $B\mathbb Z^m$. Its entries on $D'$ therefore lie in a fixed coset of $K$ and have at most $M_K(D')$ possibilities. This encodes the cross block at cost at most $\eta N(y-d')$. For the final $D'$-block, the same bound applies to each of its $d'$ rows. Ignoring symmetry within that block only enlarges the count, so its cost is at most $\eta Nd'$. Relative to an unrestricted symmetric $Y$-block the saving is at least $$\begin{split}
 \binom{y+1}{2}-\binom{y-d'+1}{2}-\eta Ny
 &=yd'-\frac{(d')^2}{2}+\frac{d'}2-\eta Ny\\
 &\ge N^2\left(xt-\frac{x^2}{2}-\eta t
                  -O(\eta^5+1/N)\right).
 \end{split}$$

It remains to compare the upper count with the multiplicity of witnesses. Group violating witnesses by $h$, the integers $t_iN$ and $xN$, and the integers $$a_i=\lfloor\log_2|S_i|\rfloor,\qquad \bar\alpha_i=a_i/N.$$ There are at most $2^{O(\eta^{-1}\log N)}$ groups. Fix one group, and let $\mathcal E$ be the set of matrices in the imposed typical intersection that have a violating witness in this group. For each $B\in\mathcal E$, select one such witness and count the distinct pairs consisting of $B$ and the basis lists supplied by that witness. Equation <a href="#eq:witness-basis-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-basis-lower">[eq:witness-basis-lower]</a> gives a lower bound of $$|\mathcal E|\,
 2^{N^2\sum_i t_i\bar\alpha_i-o(N^2\eta^4)}$$ on the number of pairs. On the other hand, Equations <a href="#eq:witness-basis-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-basis-upper">[eq:witness-basis-upper]</a> and <a href="#eq:witness-lattice-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-lattice-cost">[eq:witness-lattice-cost]</a>, together with the matrix saving, give the upper bound $$2^{T_m+
 N^2\left(\sum_i t_i^2-xt+x^2/2+\eta t\right)
 +o(N^2\eta^4)}.$$ The enumeration may count a pair more than once if it has several compatible choices of $D,P,K$; this preserves the upper bound. In particular, the lower bound selects just one witness per matrix, while the upper bound includes the union over all $K$.

Since $0\le\alpha_i-\bar\alpha_i<1/N$ and $t\le1$, violation of Equation <a href="#eq:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-pricing">[eq:witness-pricing]</a> implies $$\sum_i t_i(t_i-\bar\alpha_i)-xt+x^2/2+\eta t
 <-\eta^3+\frac1N.$$ All binary matrices have probability $2^{-T_m}$. Dividing the pair bounds therefore gives $$\Pr(\mathbf B_m\in\mathcal E)
 \le 2^{-N^2\eta^3+o(N^2\eta^4)}.$$ The group count and all omitted costs are absorbed in this error. Since $N\eta^3\to\infty$, the total probability of a violation is $2^{-\omega(N)}$, including the exceptional probabilities of the imposed rank and corank properties. This proves the assertion, including its simultaneity over the lattices, lists, and coordinate sets. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### From witness pricing to concentration

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-witness-pricing-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-witness-pricing</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:cube-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cube-concentration">31</a>.* Fix $A>0$. Choose a fixed $D_0>2A$, and put $\eta=\ell^{-D_0}$, $\rho=\eta^3$. Work on the typical event in Proposition <a href="#prop:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:witness-pricing">33</a>, and fix an arbitrary $K$ with $B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m$.

<a id="eq:cube-order-dimension"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:det-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:det-minimizer">14</a> to $K$ with $T=(\log N)^4$, and let $K_0$ be a minimizing saturated sublattice. Put $F=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}K_0$ and $$d=\frac{m-\dim F}{N},\qquad
 f=1-d=\frac{\dim F+N-m}{N}.$$ The projected quotient $K/K_0$ has rank $dN$ and all its sublattices have determinant at least $T$ to their rank. Moreover $\det K_0\le T^{\dim F}$, by comparison with the zero lattice. The projections of the columns of $B$ span $F^\perp$, and each has norm at most $\sqrt N$. Choosing $dN$ independent projections and using that their integer span is a sublattice of $K/K_0$ gives $\det(K/K_0)\le N^{dN/2}$. Hence $$\label{eq:cube-order-dimension}
 q=\frac{\log\det K}{N\log N}
 \le \frac d2+\frac{\dim F}{N}\frac{\log T}{\log N}
 \le \frac d2+\frac{4\ell}{\log N}.$$

<a id="eq:cube-fiber-extraction"></a>
Consider any nonempty subset $S$ of a coset of $K$ in the cube, and base its differences at one point of $S$. Their projections off $F$ belong to $K/K_0$ and have norm at most $\sqrt N$. Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-count">13</a> gives at most $$2^{O(N/\log^6N)}$$ distinct projections. Restrict to a largest projection fiber and then apply Lemma <a href="#lem:robust-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:robust-extraction">15</a> with parameter $\rho=\eta^3$. For sufficiently large $N$, these two operations lose at most $2\eta^3N$ bits of logarithmic size. Thus, if $|S|=2^{aN}$, they give a $\rho$-robust subset $S'$ with $$\label{eq:cube-fiber-extraction}
 |S'|\ge2^{(a-2\eta^3)N},
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{span}}(S'-S')\subset F.$$ If $S$ is supported on a coordinate set, the same is true of its differences after these operations.

<a id="eq:cube-pivot-complement"></a>
Choose a set $P_F$ of $\dim F$ pivot coordinates for $F$, so coordinate projection onto $P_F$ is injective on $F$, and put $J_0=[m]\setminus P_F$. Then $|J_0|=dN$ and a vector supported on $J_0$ belongs to $F$ only if it is zero. Distinct bit vectors supported on $J_0$ therefore have distinct projections off $F$: equal projections would give a difference in $F$. Applying the preceding projection count to a coset intersection on $J_0$ gives $$\label{eq:cube-pivot-complement}
 M_K(J_0)\le2^{O(N/\log^6N)}\le2^{\eta N}.$$

Write $M_K=2^{\alpha N}$. We have $0\le\alpha\le1$. If $\alpha<10\eta$, the conclusion follows for sufficiently large $N$, since $1-c(q)\ge0$ and $10\eta\le\ell^{-A}$. We may therefore assume $\alpha\ge10\eta$.

<a id="eq:cube-full-witness"></a>
Apply Equation <a href="#eq:cube-fiber-extraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-fiber-extraction">[eq:cube-fiber-extraction]</a> to a full-coordinate coset attaining $M_K$. Denote the resulting robust subset by $S_*$, put $W_*=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(S_*-S_*)$, and write $$|S_*|=2^{\alpha_*N},\qquad v=\frac{\dim W_*}{N}.$$ An affine space of dimension $\dim W_*$ contains at most $2^{\dim W_*}$ cube points, by coordinate projection. Consequently $$\label{eq:cube-full-witness}
 \alpha_*\ge\alpha-2\eta^3,\qquad
 \alpha-2\eta^3\le v\le\frac{\dim F}{N}\le f.$$ The last inequality uses $m\le N$.

We construct one coordinate set $D$ with small $M_K(D)$ and use it in two applications of Proposition <a href="#prop:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:witness-pricing">33</a>. A list of robust sets will force $D$ to be large; applying the estimate to the full-coordinate set $S_*$ then restricts how large $\alpha$ can be for that same $D$.

Initially all coordinates of $P_F$ are available. At step $i$, choose, among the available coordinates, a set $A_i$ of *minimum cardinality* such that $$M_K(J_0\cup A_i)>2^{\eta N}.$$ For a coset attaining $M_K(J_0\cup A_i)$, apply Equation <a href="#eq:cube-fiber-extraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-fiber-extraction">[eq:cube-fiber-extraction]</a>. It gives a robust subset $S_i$ whose difference span satisfies $$W_i\subset F\cap E_{J_0\cup A_i},\qquad
 \alpha_i=\frac{\log_2|S_i|}{N}\ge\eta-2\eta^3,\qquad
 t_i=\frac{\dim W_i}{N}\ge\alpha_i.$$ Because projection of $F$ onto $P_F$ is injective and $W_i$ vanishes on $P_F\setminus A_i$, we have $t_iN\le|A_i|$. Choose $t_iN$ pivot coordinates $H_i\subset A_i$ on which projection of $W_i$ is injective, and declare those coordinates unavailable. Stop when $t=\sum_i t_i\ge\alpha-2\eta$.

The required set $A_i$ exists before the stopping condition is met. Indeed, the coordinates already declared unavailable number $tN$. In a full coset attaining $M_K$, fix their bit values to a most frequent pattern. At least $2^{(\alpha-t)N}>2^{2\eta N}$ points remain. Subtracting the fixed pattern on those coordinates translates the coset and leaves bit vectors supported on $J_0$ and the available coordinates. Hence that coordinate set has $M_K>2^{\eta N}$. Equation <a href="#eq:cube-pivot-complement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-pivot-complement">[eq:cube-pivot-complement]</a> also shows that each $A_i$ is nonempty.

The spaces $W_i$ form a direct sum. In a relation $\sum_i w_i=0$ with $w_i\in W_i$, the later terms vanish on the pivots $H_1$; injectivity on $W_1$ gives $w_1=0$, and the same argument successively gives every $w_i=0$. Thus $t\le\dim F/N\le1$. Each increment is at least $\eta-2\eta^3$, so there are fewer than $3/\eta$ steps for sufficiently large $N$. Together with the existence just proved, this ensures that the stopping condition is reached. The family of available subsets only shrinks from one step to the next. Since each $A_i$ has minimum cardinality, the numbers $|A_i|$ do not decrease. If the last one has size $sN$, then <a id="eq:cube-coordinate-list"></a>$$\label{eq:cube-coordinate-list}
 t\ge\alpha-2\eta,\qquad t_i\le |A_i|/N\le s
 \quad\text{for every }i.$$

<a id="eq:cube-small-coordinate-set"></a>
Remove one coordinate from the last $A_i$ and include all of $J_0$. The resulting set $D$ satisfies $$\label{eq:cube-small-coordinate-set}
 |D|=(d+s)N-1,\qquad M_K(D)\le2^{\eta N},$$ by the minimum cardinality of that $A_i$. For $x\ge0$, $t>0$, define $$e(x,t)=
 \begin{cases}
 x-x^2/(2t),&0\le x\le t,\\
 t/2,&x\ge t.
 \end{cases}$$ This function is nondecreasing in both variables. Its rate of change in $x$ is between zero and one, and its rate of change in $t$ is between zero and $1/2$. These assertions follow from $$\partial_x e=1-x/t,\quad \partial_t e=x^2/(2t^2)\quad(x<t),
 \qquad
 \partial_x e=0,\quad \partial_t e=1/2\quad(x>t),$$ and continuity at $x=t$.

If necessary, truncate $D$ to a subset of size $tN$. Monotonicity of $M_K(D)$ preserves its bound, so Proposition <a href="#prop:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:witness-pricing">33</a> applied to the constructed list gives $$\begin{split}
 e(d+s,t)
 &\le \frac1t\sum_i t_i(t_i-\alpha_i)
       +\eta+\frac{\eta^3}{t}+\frac1N\\
 &\le s+O(\eta).
 \end{split}$$ The $1/N$ accounts for the coordinate removed in Equation <a href="#eq:cube-small-coordinate-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-small-coordinate-set">[eq:cube-small-coordinate-set]</a>. The last inequality uses $\alpha_i\ge0$, $t_i\le s$, and $t\ge\alpha-2\eta\ge8\eta$. Replacing $t$ by $\alpha$ keeps this bound: if $t\ge\alpha$, use monotonicity in the second variable; if $t<\alpha$, use its $1/2$-Lipschitz bound and $\alpha-t\le2\eta$. Thus $e(d+s,\alpha)\le s+O(\eta)$.

<a id="eq:cube-coordinate-lower"></a>
Put $h=d+s$. If $h\le\alpha$, the last inequality gives $$d\le \frac{h^2}{2\alpha}+O(\eta).$$ Since $\alpha\le1$, it follows that $h\ge\sqrt{2d\alpha}-O(\sqrt\eta)$. If $h\ge\alpha$, it instead gives $s\ge\alpha/2-O(\eta)$, and then $$h\ge d+\alpha/2-O(\eta)
   \ge\sqrt{2d\alpha}-O(\eta).$$ We have proved $$\label{eq:cube-coordinate-lower}
 d+s\ge\sqrt{2d\alpha}-O(\sqrt\eta).$$

<a id="eq:cube-coordinate-upper"></a>
Use the same $D$ again, now with the singleton list consisting of $S_*$. Truncate $D$ to size $vN$ if necessary. From Proposition <a href="#prop:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:witness-pricing">33</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:cube-full-witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-full-witness">[eq:cube-full-witness]</a>, $$e(d+s,v)
 \le v-\alpha_*+\eta+\frac{\eta^3}{v}+\frac1N
 \le v-\alpha+O(\eta).$$ Here $v\ge\alpha-2\eta^3\ge9\eta$ for large $N$. Since $v\le f$ and the rate of change of $e$ in its second argument is at most $1/2$, this yields $$\label{eq:cube-coordinate-upper}
 e(d+s,f)
 \le e(d+s,v)+\frac{f-v}{2}
 \le f-\alpha+O(\eta).$$

<a id="eq:cube-profile-inequality"></a>
We finish by solving the two coordinate inequalities. In the present case $f\ge v>0$. If $d\ge f$, then $d+s\ge f$, so Equation <a href="#eq:cube-coordinate-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-coordinate-upper">[eq:cube-coordinate-upper]</a> gives $$\alpha\le f/2+O(\eta)=fz(d/f)+O(\eta).$$ Suppose instead that $d\le f$. Monotonicity and the unit Lipschitz bound of $e$ in its first argument, together with Equations <a href="#eq:cube-coordinate-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-coordinate-lower">[eq:cube-coordinate-lower]</a> and <a href="#eq:cube-coordinate-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-coordinate-upper">[eq:cube-coordinate-upper]</a>, give $$\label{eq:cube-profile-inequality}
 e(\sqrt{2d\alpha},f)\le f-\alpha+O(\sqrt\eta).$$ Put $u=d/f\in[0,1]$. Directly from Equation <a href="#eq:cube-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-profile">[eq:cube-profile]</a>, $$2uz(u)\le1,\qquad
 1-z(u)=\sqrt{2uz(u)}-uz(u).$$ For completeness, if $r=\sqrt{2u-u^2}$, then $(1-u)^2z(u)^2-2z(u)+1=0$, which gives the second identity after taking the nonnegative square root. The first is equivalent to $2u\le1+r$; for $u\le1/2$ it is immediate, and for $u\ge1/2$ its square follows from $r^2-(2u-1)^2=(1-u)(5u-1)\ge0$. These identities show that $\alpha_0=fz(u)$ satisfies $$e(\sqrt{2d\alpha_0},f)=f-\alpha_0.$$ The function $a\mapsto a+e(\sqrt{2da},f)$ is nondecreasing and increases by at least the increase in $a$. Therefore Equation <a href="#eq:cube-profile-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-profile-inequality">[eq:cube-profile-inequality]</a> implies $\alpha\le\alpha_0+O(\sqrt\eta)$. Both cases give $$\alpha\le fz(d/f)+O(\sqrt\eta)
          =1-c(d/2)+O(\sqrt\eta).$$

By Equation <a href="#eq:cube-order-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-order-dimension">[eq:cube-order-dimension]</a>, monotonicity of $c$, and Equation <a href="#eq:cube-holder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-holder">[eq:cube-holder]</a>, $$c(q)\le c(d/2)+O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\log N}}\right).$$ It follows that $$\alpha\le 1-c(q)+O(\sqrt\eta)
                    +O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\log N}}\right).$$ Because $D_0>2A$ is fixed, both error terms are $o(\ell^{-A})$. Increasing $N$ makes their sum at most $\ell^{-A}$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:cube-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-concentration">[eq:cube-concentration]</a>. The argument was deterministic for every $K$ on the single typical event of Proposition <a href="#prop:witness-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:witness-pricing">33</a>, so the conclusion is simultaneous in $K$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificate-pricing"></a>
### Pricing retained layers by certificates

Retaining layers in a quotient imposes a lattice condition on the new column. We now count partial certificates of that condition. The resulting estimate permits the certificate size to be chosen separately in each class of outcomes; this will allow a later argument to choose it according to the largest potential in that class.

We first record the probability bound for a fixed certificate at any fixed cube precision $A_{\mathrm{cube}}>0$. Let $\mathcal F$ be exposed information that fixes a nonsingular binary matrix $C$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ and a quotient map $\pi:\mathbb Z^m\twoheadrightarrow G$ with $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\ker\pi$. Suppose that, conditionally on $\mathcal F$, a column $v$ is uniform on $\{0,1\}^m$. Take $C$ in the typical set of Proposition <a href="#prop:cube-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cube-concentration">31</a> at precision $A_{\mathrm{cube}}$.

<a id="eq:fixed-certificate-probability"></a>
Fix a candidate set $E$ of positive layers of $G$ after these data are fixed, and let $\mathcal R$ be the layers retained on quotienting $G$ by $\pi(v)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:retention-lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:retention-lattice">29</a> gives a lattice $K_E$, fixed by $\pi$ and $E$, such that $E\subseteq\mathcal R$ implies $v\in K_E$, with $$C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K_E\subseteq\mathbb Z^m,\qquad
 [\mathbb Z^m:K_E]=N^{Nq(E)},\qquad
 q(E)=\sum_{(p,e)\in E}k_G(p,e)\nu_p.$$ The group-order bound in Equation <a href="#eq:group-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:group-order">[eq:group-order]</a> gives $0\le q(E)\le1/2$. Conditional uniformity of $v$ and the simultaneous cube estimate therefore give $$\label{eq:fixed-certificate-probability}
 \begin{aligned}
 \Pr(E\subseteq\mathcal R\mid\mathcal F)
 &\le \Pr(v\in K_E\mid\mathcal F)
 \le 2^{-m}M_{K_E}\\
 &\le 2^{-m}2^{N(1-c(q(E))+\ell^{-A_{\mathrm{cube}}})}
 =2^{-N(c(q(E))-\eta_N)},\\
 \eta_N&=\ell^{-A_{\mathrm{cube}}}+1-\frac mN.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $1-m/N\le N^{-3/10}$. The same typical set works for every exposed quotient, since all its certificate lattices contain the original $C\mathbb Z^m$. We will handle marks that depend on $v$ by counting the fixed candidates and how many each outcome offers.

<a id="eq:entropy-cost"></a>
For $0\le t\le1$, let $$H_2(t)=-t\log_2t-(1-t)\log_2(1-t),
 \qquad H_2(0)=H_2(1)=0.$$ For $s,u\ge0$ and $0\le x\le s$, define $$\label{eq:entropy-cost}
 \mathcal E(s,u,x)
 =(s+u)H_2\!\left(\frac{x}{s+u}\right)
       -sH_2\!\left(\frac{x}{s}\right),$$ using the continuous values when a denominator vanishes. Here $s$ and $u$ will be the total weights of marked and unmarked layers, and $x$ will be a target weight for a certificate chosen among the marked layers. When all layers have weight $1/N$, the ratio $\binom{(s+u)N}{xN}/\binom{sN}{xN}$ counts possible certificates relative to those offered by a fixed marked set, and its base-two logarithm is $N\mathcal E(s,u,x)+O(\log N)$ for integer counts.

<a id="stmt-prop-certificate-pricing"></a>
<div id="prop:certificate-pricing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 34** (Certificate pricing). *Fix $A>0$ and at most two positive constants $w_i$. Typically for nonsingular binary matrices $C$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, the following assertion holds. The typical set is obtained by imposing Proposition <a href="#prop:cube-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cube-concentration">31</a> at a sufficiently large fixed precision depending on $A$ and these constants.*

*Let $\mathcal F$ denote exposed information which fixes $C$, a quotient map $G_C\twoheadrightarrow G$, and the data described next. Suppose that, conditionally on $\mathcal F$, a tested column $v$ is uniform on $\{0,1\}^m$. Partition the layers of $G$ into at most two categories, each a union of whole plateaus. In category $i$, assume that every height is at least $w_i$; only $w_i=1,2$ will be needed. The partition is part of the exposed data. In the quotient of $G$ by the image of $v$, an outcome may mark some of the retained layers. Require that the marked layers on each plateau form a prefix. The marks may also depend on other random bits in the experiment.*

<a id="eq:certificate-pricing"></a>
*Let $\mathcal A$ be any subevent of this experiment. There is a partition of $\mathcal A$ into at most $2^{N\ell^{-A}}$ classes, constructed before choosing a representative or any target weights, with the following property. Take any nonempty class $\mathcal A_\alpha$, any representative outcome in it, and write $s_i$ and $u_i$ for the representative’s sums of the weights $\nu_p$ of the marked and unmarked layers in category $i$. For any choices $0\le x_i\le s_i$, $$\label{eq:certificate-pricing}
 \Pr(\mathcal A_\alpha\mid\mathcal F)
 \le 2^{-N\left(
 c\left(\sum_i w_i x_i\right)
       -\sum_i\mathcal E(s_i,u_i,x_i)-\ell^{-A}\right)}.$$ Here $\mathcal A_\alpha$ already includes the restriction to $\mathcal A$; the probability is conditioned only on the exposed information. The class count and the estimate are uniform in that information, the quotient, the subevent, and the representative. In particular, the exposed information may include a preceding column that fixes $G$, provided the tested column remains conditionally uniform.*

</div>

<a id="eq:entropy-difference"></a>
<a id="eq:entropy-superadditive"></a>
We record the entropy facts needed below. Put $$H(s,u)=(s+u)H_2\!\left(\frac{s}{s+u}\right),\qquad H(0,0)=0.$$ Expansion of the logarithms gives $$\label{eq:entropy-difference}
 \mathcal E(s,u,x)=H(s,u)-H(s-x,u).$$ For $s>0$, $$\frac{\partial H}{\partial s}(s,u)=\log_2(1+u/s),$$ which is nonnegative and nonincreasing in $s$. Thus $\mathcal E\ge0$. For fixed $s,u$, the function $\mathcal E(s,u,x)$ is convex in $x\in[0,s]$: in the interior its derivative is $\log_2(1+u/(s-x))$, which is nondecreasing in $x$. For fixed $u,x$, it is nonincreasing in $s\ge x$, since for $s>x$, $$\frac{\partial\mathcal E}{\partial s}(s,u,x)
 =\log_2(1+u/s)-\log_2(1+u/(s-x))\le0.$$ The endpoint cases follow by continuity. The defining formula also gives homogeneity of degree one in all three size variables: $\mathcal E(ts,tu,tx)=t\mathcal E(s,u,x)$ for $t\ge0$. For fixed $0\le\theta\le1$, write $$\mathcal E_\theta(s,u)=\mathcal E(s,u,\theta s).$$ This function is concave and homogeneous of degree one in $(s,u)$. Indeed, the first term in $$\mathcal E_\theta(s,u)
 =(s+u)H_2\!\left(\frac{\theta s}{s+u}\right)-sH_2(\theta)$$ is the perspective of the concave function $H_2$, composed with a linear map, and the second term is linear. Consequently $\mathcal E_\theta$ is superadditive on the nonnegative quadrant: $$\label{eq:entropy-superadditive}
 \sum_j\mathcal E_\theta(s_j,u_j)
 \le \mathcal E_\theta\!\left(\sum_j s_j,\sum_j u_j\right).$$

These functions have uniform continuity estimates at their boundaries. For every fixed $M$, the function $-t\log_2t$, extended by zero at zero, is Hölder continuous of exponent $1/2$ on $[0,M]$. To see this, integrate its derivative on an interval of length $d$; the absolute integral is at most $O_M(d(1+|\log d|))=O_M(\sqrt d)$, with the same conclusion for intervals meeting zero. Expressing $H(s,u)$ as $$-s\log_2s-u\log_2u+(s+u)\log_2(s+u)$$ therefore shows that it is Hölder continuous of exponent $1/2$ on every bounded nonnegative rectangle. Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-difference">[eq:entropy-difference]</a> gives the same estimate for $\mathcal E(s,u,x)$ on bounded parts of its domain. It also gives an estimate for $\mathcal E_\theta(s,u)$ whose constant is uniform over $0\le\theta\le1$. We will use these estimates both to round certificate cardinalities and to replace layer weights by nearby cell weights.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-certificate-pricing"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-certificate-pricing</summary>

*Proof.* Work conditionally on fixed exposed data satisfying the assumptions. Choose a fixed cube precision $A_{\mathrm{cube}}>0$, to be specified at the end, and invoke Equation <a href="#eq:fixed-certificate-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-certificate-probability">[eq:fixed-certificate-probability]</a> for each fixed candidate $E$. Here the quotient map $G_C\twoheadrightarrow G$, composed with the natural map from $\mathbb Z^m$, gives the exposed map $\pi$ in that observation. This uses only the conditional uniformity of $v$; additional random bits and the restriction to $\mathcal A$ need not be independent of $v$.

<a id="eq:certificate-order-bounds"></a>
We now construct the classes. Fix a large constant $D>0$, to be chosen at the end, and set $\delta=\ell^{-D}$ and $a_p=\log_Np$. The order bound gives $$\label{eq:certificate-order-bounds}
 \sum_{p,e}k_G(p,e)\frac{a_p}{N}\le\frac12,
 \qquad
 \sum_{p,e}k_G(p,e)\le\frac{N\log N}{2\log2}.$$ We classify the marks in three prime ranges.

For $a_p>\ell^D$, record every mark bit. There are at most $N/(2\ell^D)$ layers in this range by the first bound in Equation <a href="#eq:certificate-order-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-order-bounds">[eq:certificate-order-bounds]</a>, so this record costs $O(N\ell^{-D})$ bits.

For $a_p<1-\delta$, record the number of marks on each plateau. These counts determine all the marks because they are prefixes. There are at most $N^{1-\delta}$ primes in this range. If $d_p$ is the number of plateaus at $p$, their distinct positive heights give $$\frac{d_p(d_p+1)}2\le\sum_e k_G(p,e).$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and the second bound in Equation <a href="#eq:certificate-order-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-order-bounds">[eq:certificate-order-bounds]</a> show that the total number of these plateaus is $O(N^{1-\delta/2}\sqrt{\log N})$. Each prefix count costs $O(\log N)$ bits, since every plateau length is $O(N\log N)$. The total cost is therefore $$O\!\left(N^{1-\delta/2}(\log N)^{3/2}\right)
       =o(N\ell^{-D}).$$ For the last equality, use $\delta\log N=(\log N)/\ell^D\gg\log\log N$.

The remaining primes satisfy $1-\delta\le a_p\le\ell^D$. For each category, split this range into bins of width at most $\delta$, starting at $1-\delta$, and call a category-bin pair a cell. There are $J=O(\ell^{2D})$ cells. In a cell $j$, let $\lambda_j$ be the lower endpoint of its bin, so $$1-\delta\le\lambda_j\le\ell^D,
 \qquad \lambda_j\le a_p\le\lambda_j+\delta
 \quad\hbox{for its layers}.$$ The number of layers in this entire range is $O(N)$, again by the first order bound. Record only the number of marks in each cell. This costs $O(\ell^{2D}\log N)=o(N\ell^{-D})$ bits. All these records depend only on the marks and the exposed data; intersecting their classes with $\mathcal A$ gives the desired classes for the subevent. Their number is at most $2^{O(N\ell^{-D})+o(N\ell^{-D})}$, uniformly in the exposed quotient.

Fix one class, a representative, and target weights as in the statement. For category $i$, put $\theta_i=x_i/s_i$ if $s_i>0$, and put $\theta_i=0$ otherwise. In the large and small prime ranges the marked sets are known throughout the class; include all of them in each certificate. In an intermediate cell $j$ of category $i$, let $n_j$ be its number of layers and $r_j$ its recorded number of marks, and require a certificate to contain any $$a_j=\lfloor\theta_i r_j\rfloor$$ of those marked layers. Thus every outcome in the class offers exactly $$\mathfrak m=\prod_j\binom{r_j}{a_j}$$ certificates, whereas the number of possible certificates for the class is at most $$\mathfrak n=\prod_j\binom{n_j}{a_j}.$$ Empty products have value one. The latter count is an upper bound because it allows arbitrary layers in each cell, without imposing the prefix restriction.

<a id="eq:certificate-index-lower-bound"></a>
We check the weight certified by each such choice uniformly over all outcomes in the class. In category $i$, let $s_i^0$ be the marked weight in the large and small prime ranges; it is fixed in the class. The representative’s marked weight in its intermediate cells differs from $\sum_{j\in i}\lambda_jr_j/N$ by at most $\delta\sum_{j\in i}r_j/N=O(\delta)$. The weight selected in those cells is at least $$\sum_{j\in i}\frac{\lambda_j a_j}{N}
 \ge \theta_i\sum_{j\in i}\frac{\lambda_j r_j}{N}
              -\frac1N\sum_{j\in i}\lambda_j.$$ The last term is $O(\ell^{3D}/N)=o(\delta)$. Since all of the known marked weight is included and $s_i^0\ge\theta_i s_i^0$, the selected weight in this category is at least $x_i-O(\delta)$. Every selected layer there has height at least $w_i$. Consequently, uniformly over its choices, $$\label{eq:certificate-index-lower-bound}
 q(E)\ge q_0-O(\delta),\qquad q_0=\sum_i w_i x_i.$$ Both $q(E)$ and $q_0$ belong to $[0,1/2]$: for the latter, $q_0\le\sum_i w_i s_i\le\sum_{p,e}k_G(p,e)\nu_p$. The profile $c$ is increasing and Hölder continuous of exponent $1/2$ on this interval. Equation <a href="#eq:certificate-index-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-index-lower-bound">[eq:certificate-index-lower-bound]</a> therefore gives $$c(q(E))\ge c(q_0)-O(\sqrt\delta).$$ Together with Equation <a href="#eq:fixed-certificate-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-certificate-probability">[eq:fixed-certificate-probability]</a>, this bounds the probability of offering any one fixed candidate certificate by $2^{-N(c(q_0)-\eta_N-O(\sqrt\delta))}$. This conclusion holds after intersection with the class and with $\mathcal A$, since such an intersection still implies $v\in K_E$.

<a id="eq:certificate-double-count"></a>
Double counting outcome-certificate pairs now yields $$\label{eq:certificate-double-count}
 \Pr(\mathcal A_\alpha\mid\mathcal F)
 \le \frac{\mathfrak n}{\mathfrak m}
       2^{-N(c(q_0)-\eta_N-O(\sqrt\delta))}.$$ Indeed, summing over candidate certificates the probabilities of the outcomes in the class offering that certificate gives $\mathfrak m\Pr(\mathcal A_\alpha\mid\mathcal F)$; each summand is bounded by the fixed-certificate estimate. This also explains why no conditional independence of the marks or of the subevent is needed.

It remains to bound the ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:certificate-double-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-double-count">[eq:certificate-double-count]</a>. For integers $0\le a\le n$, the elementary binomial bounds are $$nH_2(a/n)-\log_2(n+1)
 \le\log_2\binom na\le nH_2(a/n).$$ For completeness, insert the parameter $a/n$ into the binomial distribution. The upper bound follows because its probability at $a$ is at most one, and the lower bound follows because $a$ is a mode and there are $n+1$ probabilities summing to one. The endpoints are immediate. Applying these bounds to a cell gives $$\frac1N\log_2\frac{\binom{n_j}{a_j}}{\binom{r_j}{a_j}}
 \le
 \mathcal E\!\left(\frac{r_j}{N},\frac{n_j-r_j}{N},\frac{a_j}{N}\right)
       +\frac{\log_2(r_j+1)}N.$$ All arguments here stay in a fixed bounded set. Replacing $a_j/N$ by $\theta_i r_j/N$ changes the entropy term by $O(N^{-1/2})$, uniformly in $\theta_i$, by the Hölder estimate above. After summing over $J=O(\ell^{2D})$ cells, all rounding and binomial errors are $$O\!\left(\ell^{2D}\left(N^{-1/2}+\frac{\log N}{N}\right)\right),$$ which is smaller than every fixed negative power of $\ell$.

<a id="eq:certificate-entropy-bound"></a>
We convert the remaining entropy terms from counts to weights. Their nonnegativity and homogeneity give, for a cell in category $i$, $$\mathcal E_{\theta_i}\!\left(\frac{r_j}{N},\frac{n_j-r_j}{N}\right)
 \le\frac1{1-\delta}
 \mathcal E_{\theta_i}\!\left(
       \frac{\lambda_jr_j}{N},\frac{\lambda_j(n_j-r_j)}{N}\right).$$ Let $u_i^0$, alongside $s_i^0$, denote the unmarked weight in the known large and small ranges, and put $$\underline s_i=s_i^0+\sum_{j\in i}\frac{\lambda_jr_j}{N},
 \qquad
 \underline u_i=u_i^0+\sum_{j\in i}\frac{\lambda_j(n_j-r_j)}{N}.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-superadditive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-superadditive">[eq:entropy-superadditive]</a> the weighted cell terms sum to at most $\mathcal E_{\theta_i}(\underline s_i,\underline u_i)$. Here adding the known ranges preserves this upper bound because their entropy term is nonnegative. The representative weights satisfy $$0\le s_i-\underline s_i=O(\delta),\qquad
 0\le u_i-\underline u_i=O(\delta).$$ All these weights have bounded sum, at most $1/2$ before replacing weights by lower endpoints. The uniform Hölder estimate for $\mathcal E_{\theta_i}$ and the factor $(1-\delta)^{-1}=1+O(\delta)$ therefore show that $$\label{eq:certificate-entropy-bound}
 \frac1N\log_2\frac{\mathfrak n}{\mathfrak m}
 \le \sum_i\mathcal E(s_i,u_i,x_i)+O(\sqrt\delta)
   +O\!\left(\ell^{2D}\left(N^{-1/2}+\frac{\log N}{N}\right)\right).$$ The constants in this estimate are uniform in the targets, including their endpoint values.

Choose the fixed $D$ sufficiently large that $\ell^{-D/2}=o(\ell^{-A})$, and then choose a fixed cube precision $A_{\mathrm{cube}}>A$. The class count is at most $2^{N\ell^{-A}}$ for large $N$. Moreover, $\eta_N$, the rounding errors, and the two $O(\sqrt\delta)$ errors in Equations <a href="#eq:certificate-double-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-double-count">[eq:certificate-double-count]</a> and <a href="#eq:certificate-entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-entropy-bound">[eq:certificate-entropy-bound]</a> have sum at most $\ell^{-A}$ for large $N$. Substitution proves Equation <a href="#eq:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:certificate-pricing">[eq:certificate-pricing]</a>. Every choice of precision was fixed before $N$ tended to infinity and was independent of the quotient and exposed columns, as required. ◻

</details>

We next extract a uniform cost for retaining a noticeable total weight. Set <a id="eq:retention-scales"></a>$$\label{eq:retention-scales}
 R=\lceil\ell^2\rceil,\qquad \sigma=R^{-6}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-retention-cost"></a>
<div id="prop:retention-cost" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:retention-cost"></a>
**Proposition 35** (Retained-layer cost). *Typically for nonsingular binary matrices $C$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, the following estimate holds. Let $h\in\{1,2\}$ and let $v_1,\ldots,v_h$ be independent uniform columns in $\{0,1\}^m$, conditionally on $C$. Starting with $G^{(0)}=G_C$, let $G^{(j)}$ be the quotient of $G^{(j-1)}$ by the image of $v_j$. For each $1\le j\le h$, and conditionally on any values of the preceding columns, $$\label{eq:retention-cost}
 \Pr\!\left(
  \sum_{\substack{(p,e)\text{ retained in}\\
                  G^{(j-1)}\twoheadrightarrow G^{(j)}}}\nu_p
       \ge\sigma
       \,\middle|\, C,v_1,\ldots,v_{j-1}\right)
 \le 2^{-N\sigma/2}.$$ The typical set imposes one fixed precision on $C$, independent of the values of the preceding columns. When these columns occur in a random enlargement of $C$, no nonsingularity assumption on the destination matrix is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-retention-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-retention-cost</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificate-pricing">34</a> at the fixed precision $A_0=40$. For the $j$-th quotient, condition on $C$ and the preceding columns; these fix the quotient map to $G^{(j-1)}$, while $v_j$ remains uniform. Use one category of all its layers, take $w_1=1$, and mark all retained layers. Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-quotient">28</a> ensures that these marks are prefixes on plateaus. Restrict to the subevent in Equation <a href="#eq:retention-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retention-cost">[eq:retention-cost]</a>. For any representative of a nonempty class its marked weight $s$ is at least $\sigma$, so we may choose $x=\sigma^2$. The total layer weight $s+u$ is at most $1/2$, by Equation <a href="#eq:group-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:group-order">[eq:group-order]</a>. Since $H_2(t)\le t\log_2(e/t)$, Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-cost">[eq:entropy-cost]</a> yields $$0\le\mathcal E(s,u,\sigma^2)
 \le (s+u)H_2\!\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{s+u}\right)
 \le O\!\left(\sigma^2\log(1/\sigma)\right).$$ This bound is uniform over the representatives in this subevent.

The defining formula for the profile $c$ satisfies $c(q)=2\sqrt q+O(q)$ as $q\downarrow0$. Indeed, in that formula $u=2q/(1-2q)=2q+O(q^2)$, so $\sqrt{2u-u^2}=2\sqrt q+O(q^{3/2})$, and substitution gives the claimed expansion. In particular $c(q)\ge(3/2)\sqrt q$ for all sufficiently small $q$. Proposition <a href="#prop:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificate-pricing">34</a> therefore bounds the probability of each class by $$2^{-N\left((3/2)\sigma
       -O(\sigma^2\log(1/\sigma))-\ell^{-40}\right)}.$$ There are at most $2^{N\ell^{-40}}$ classes. Since $\sigma\asymp\ell^{-12}$, both $\sigma^2\log(1/\sigma)=o(\sigma)$ and $\ell^{-40}=o(\sigma)$. Summing the class probabilities gives Equation <a href="#eq:retention-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retention-cost">[eq:retention-cost]</a> for large $N$.

The argument tests only the quotient by the selected column. It does not use a new principal block or any property of an enlarged matrix. The conditional uniformity for a second column and the simultaneous cube estimate for lattices containing $C\mathbb Z^m$ establish the claimed uniformity over the preceding column. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:potential-step"></a>
### Paying for an increase of potential

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a>. For a transition of width one, two bounds on the orders of the endpoint groups restrict the possible layer changes. A scalar optimization then shows that the certificate cost pays for the increase of potential. For width two, the gate is paid for directly by the admissible-column estimate.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Layer changes and order budgets

Fix a transition of width one from a nonsingular matrix $C$ to a nonsingular matrix $B$, and let $Q$ be the common quotient obtained from $G_C$ by the new column and from $G_B$ by the new coordinate. For an old layer $(p,e)$, write $$k=k_C(p,e),\qquad k'=k_Q(p,e).$$ The interlacing of layer heights gives $$k'\in\{k-1,k\},\qquad k_B(p,e)\in\{k',k'+1\}.$$ If $k_C(p,e)=0$, the same statement gives $k_B(p,e)\le1$, so this layer contributes nothing to either potential.

Use the weights $\nu_p$ in all sums below. In the height-one category, mark exactly the layers that reach height two; in the category of old height at least two, mark every retained layer. Let $g,v_0$ be the marked and unmarked weights in the first category, and $a,u$ those in the second. The following six types define $L$ and $P$; the last column bounds the potential change per unit weight.

<div class="center">

|                   |                  |                     |        |           |
|:-----------------:|:----------------:|:-------------------:|:------:|:---------:|
|      weight       |                  |                     |        |           |
|      height       | retention status |                     |        |           |
|      height       |                  |                     |        |           |
| per unit, at most |                  |                     |        |           |
|        $g$        |       $1$        |      retained       |  $2$   |  $\beta$  |
|       $v_0$       |       $1$        | retention may occur | $\le1$ |    $0$    |
|       $a-L$       |     $k\ge2$      |      retained       | $k+1$  | $6-\beta$ |
|        $L$        |     $k\ge2$      |      retained       |  $k$   |    $0$    |
|       $u-P$       |     $k\ge2$      |    not retained     | $k-1$  | $-\beta$  |
|        $P$        |     $k\ge2$      |    not retained     |  $k$   |    $0$    |

</div>

The $g$-row is retained because $k_B(p,e)=2$ forces $k'=1$. The potential bounds follow from $\phi(2)=\beta=7/5$ and $\phi(k)=2k$ for $k\ge3$: an increase from height at least two costs at most $6-\beta$ per unit weight, and a decrease saves at least $\beta$. Layers that stay at the same height make no change.

Both categories consist of whole plateaus determined by $G_C$. The marks are prefixes on these plateaus: this is the retention property for the second category, and for the first it follows from the fact that $k_B(p,e)$ is nonincreasing in $e$. Thus these marks have the form required in Proposition <a href="#prop:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificate-pricing">34</a>.

We claim <a id="eq:flat-compensation"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-compensation}
 P\le L,\qquad a_0:=a-L+P\in[0,a].$$ Here $a_0$ is an effective scalar mass; the actual marked family in the second category has mass $a$. Thus every $0\le y\le a_0\le a$ is a valid target for that marked family. To prove the first assertion, fix $p$ and let $t$ be the last exponent at which $k_C(p,e)\ge2$, taking $t=0$ if there is none. Attach a subscript $p$ to the contribution of this prime to each weight sum. In the notation of Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-inequality">30</a>, a retained layer in this range increases the height by one unless it is flat; an unmarked layer decreases it by one unless it persists. Consequently that Lemma, with $h=1$, gives $$(a_p-L_p)-(u_p-P_p)
 =\nu_p\bigl(F_B(p,t)-F_C(p,t)\bigr)
 \le \nu_p(t-2U_t)=a_p-u_p.$$ The last equality holds because $U_t$ counts the unmarked layers in this range. Hence $P_p\le L_p$, and summing over primes proves $P\le L$. Also $L\le a$, so $a_0=a-L+P$ is nonnegative and at most $a$.

<a id="eq:potential-increment"></a>
Summing the potential bounds in the table gives $$\label{eq:potential-increment}
 \begin{aligned}
 \Psi(B)-\Psi(C)
 &\le \beta g+(6-\beta)(a-L)-\beta(u-P)\\
 &\le I:=\beta g+(6-\beta)a_0-\beta u.
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, the first line equals $I-(6-2\beta)P$, and $6-2\beta>0$.

The total height times weight is at most $1/2$ at both endpoints. At the $B$ endpoint it is at least $$2g+3(a-L)+2L+(u-P)+2P
 =2g+3a+u-L+P
 \ge 2g+3a_0+u.$$ At the $C$ endpoint it is at least $g+v_0+2a+2u$, which is at least $g+v_0+2a_0+2u$. We have therefore obtained the two budgets <a id="eq:potential-budgets"></a>$$\label{eq:potential-budgets}
 2g+3a_0+u\le\frac12,\qquad
 g+v_0+2a_0+2u\le\frac12.$$

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Scalar bound and conditional potential estimate

<a id="stmt-lem-budget-optimization"></a>
<div id="lem:budget-optimization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:budget-primal"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Budget optimization). *Let $c$ and $\mathcal E$ be defined by Equations <a href="#eq:cube-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-profile">[eq:cube-profile]</a> and <a href="#eq:entropy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-cost">[eq:entropy-cost]</a>, and let $\beta=7/5$. If $g,v_0,a_0,u\ge0$ satisfy $$2g+3a_0+u\le\frac12,\qquad
 g+v_0+2a_0+2u\le\frac12,$$ then there are $x\in[0,g]$ and $y\in[0,a_0]$ for which $$\label{eq:budget-primal}
 c(x+2y)-\mathcal E(g,v_0,x)-\mathcal E(a_0,u,y)
 \ge \beta g+(6-\beta)a_0-\beta u.$$*

</div>

The inequality says that certificate concentration, after paying both entropy costs, covers the upper bound $I$ for the potential increase. The proof of the lemma follows in the next subsection.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-budget-optimization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-budget-optimization</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a>.* Fix the desired precision $A>0$, and set $A_1=A+2$. For width one, take a nonsingular starting matrix $C$ in the typical set of Proposition <a href="#prop:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificate-pricing">34</a> at precision $A_1$ for $(w_1,w_2)=(1,2)$. Extend the marking rule above to singular outcomes by leaving all layers unmarked. Apply that Proposition with exposed information $C$, starting quotient $G_C$, and the subevent $\{\det B\ne0\}$. The tested column is uniform conditional on $C$, and the marks defined above are allowed to depend also on the new diagonal bit. The Proposition gives at most $2^{N\ell^{-A_1}}$ classes. In each nonempty class $\mathcal C$, choose an outcome $B_{\mathcal C}$ maximizing $\Psi(B)$ in that class. Such a representative exists because the outcome space is finite.

For this representative, form $g,v_0,a,u,L,P,a_0$ and $I$ as above. Lemma <a href="#lem:budget-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:budget-optimization">36</a> supplies $x\in[0,g]$ and $y\in[0,a_0]$. These are admissible certificate target weights for the categories of minimum heights one and two, since $a_0\le a$. The monotonicity of $\mathcal E$ in its first argument gives $$\begin{aligned}
 c(x+2y)-\mathcal E(g,v_0,x)-\mathcal E(a,u,y)
 &\ge c(x+2y)-\mathcal E(g,v_0,x)-\mathcal E(a_0,u,y)\\
 &\ge I.
 \end{aligned}$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:certificate-pricing">34</a>, applied to this representative in the restricted outcome space, therefore yields $$\Pr(\mathcal C\mid C)\le2^{-N(I-\ell^{-A_1})}.$$ Since $\Psi(B_{\mathcal C})-\Psi(C)\le I$ by Equation <a href="#eq:potential-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:potential-increment">[eq:potential-increment]</a>, the contribution of this class satisfies $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal C}\,2^{N\Psi(B)}\mid C\right]
 \le 2^{N\Psi(B_{\mathcal C})}\Pr(\mathcal C\mid C)
 \le 2^{N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A_1})}.$$ Summing over the classes proves $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\det B\ne0\}}\,2^{N\Psi(B)}\mid C\right]
 \le2^{N(\Psi(C)+2\ell^{-A_1})}
 \le2^{N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A})}$$ for all sufficiently large $N$.

For width two, take $C$ also in the typical set of Proposition <a href="#prop:admissible-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:admissible-potential">7</a> at precision $A_1$, and let $m$ be its size. Conditional on $C$, the two cross columns $v_1,v_2$ are independent and uniform on $\{0,1\}^m$. For the gate $\mathcal G_2=\{v_1,v_2\in\mathcal S(C)\}$, that Proposition gives $$\Pr(\mathcal G_2\mid C)
 =2^{-2m}|\mathcal S(C)|^2
 \le2^{-2m+2N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A_1})}.$$ On nonsingular outcomes $\Psi(B)\le1$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
