 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\det B\ne0\}}\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G_2}
                2^{N\Psi(B)}\mid C\right]
 &\le2^{N-2m+2N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A_1})}\\
 &\le2^{N(\Psi(C)+2(1-m/N)+2\ell^{-A_1})}\\
 &\le2^{N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-A})}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The second line uses $\Psi(C)\le1$, and the last uses $1-m/N\le N^{-3/10}=o(\ell^{-A})$ together with $A_1=A+2$. Intersecting the two typical sets proves both conditional assertions of Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a>, with arbitrary fixed precision and with the stated nonsingularity and gate indicators. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Proof of the budget optimization

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-budget-optimization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-budget-optimization</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:budget-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:budget-optimization">36</a>.* The second budget ensures $x+2y\le1/2$ throughout the rectangle under consideration. Sections <a href="#sec:cube" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cube">8</a> and <a href="#sec:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate-pricing">9</a> show that $c$ is nondecreasing and concave, while $\mathcal E$ is continuous, nonnegative, convex in its target, homogeneous of degree one in all size variables, and nonincreasing in its first argument for fixed $u,x$. Equation <a href="#eq:cube-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-profile">[eq:cube-profile]</a> further shows that $c$ is differentiable on $(0,1/2)$, including the join at $q=1/4$, has left derivative $1$ at $1/2$, and satisfies $c(q)/q\to\infty$ as $q\downarrow0$.

<a id="eq:budget-dual"></a>
**Supporting slope.** For $w,t\ge0$, put $$c^*(w)=\max_{0\le q\le1/2}\bigl(c(q)-wq\bigr),\qquad
 \mathcal F_t(s,u)=\max_{0\le x\le s}
       \bigl(tx-\mathcal E(s,u,x)\bigr).$$ Write $I=\beta g+(6-\beta)a_0-\beta u$. We will prove that, for every $w\ge0$, $$\label{eq:budget-dual}
 c^*(w)+\mathcal F_w(g,v_0)+\mathcal F_{2w}(a_0,u)\ge I.$$ Here is a justification that this suffices. If $g=a_0=0$, taking $x=y=0$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:budget-primal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-primal">[eq:budget-primal]</a> immediately. Otherwise let $(x_*,y_*)$ maximize its left side on the compact rectangle, and put $q_*=x_*+2y_*$. At zero the objective is zero. For a positive one of $g,a_0$, its entropy cost is $O(x)$ or $O(y)$ near zero, by the derivative of $H$ recorded in Section <a href="#sec:certificate-pricing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate-pricing">9</a>; the fact that $c(q)/q\to\infty$ then shows that the maximum is positive. Thus $q_*>0$.

Take $w=c'(q_*)$ if $q_*<1/2$, and the left derivative if $q_*=1/2$. This is a nonnegative supporting slope, so $c^*(w)=c(q_*)-wq_*$. Let $$K(x,y)=\mathcal E(g,v_0,x)+\mathcal E(a_0,u,y).$$ We claim that $(x_*,y_*)$ maximizes $w(x+2y)-K(x,y)$ on the same rectangle. If some $(x,y)$ gave a strictly larger value, take the point a fraction $\theta$ along the segment from $(x_*,y_*)$ to $(x,y)$. Convexity of $K$ and the one-sided differentiability of $c$ in this feasible direction give, as $\theta\downarrow0$, an increase in the original objective of at least $$\theta\bigl(w(x+2y-q_*)-K(x,y)+K(x_*,y_*)\bigr)+o(\theta)>0,$$ a contradiction. The claimed maximum separates into its two coordinates. Hence the maximum of the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:budget-primal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-primal">[eq:budget-primal]</a> is exactly $c^*(w)+\mathcal F_w(g,v_0)+\mathcal F_{2w}(a_0,u)$ at this supporting slope. Equation <a href="#eq:budget-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-dual">[eq:budget-dual]</a> proves the desired assertion.

<a id="eq:entropy-dual-minimum"></a>
**Entropy minimum and criterion.** We use the function $H$ from Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-difference">[eq:entropy-difference]</a> to compute a lower bound for the two $\mathcal F$ terms. For $t>0$ set $$D(t)=-\log_2(1-2^{-t}).$$ For $r\ge0$, maximizing $H(z,r)-tz$ over $0\le z\le1$ at $z=\min\{1,r/(2^t-1)\}$ gives $$\mathcal F_t(1,r)=
 \begin{cases}
 t-H(1,r)+D(t)r,&0\le r\le2^t-1,\\
 0,&r\ge2^t-1.
 \end{cases}$$ For $h\ge0$, the derivative on the first branch after adding $hr$ is $D(t)+h-\log_2(1+1/r)$. It is increasing in $r$ and vanishes at $r=(2^{D(t)+h}-1)^{-1}\le2^t-1$, with equality when $h=0$. On the second branch $hr$ is nondecreasing. Evaluation at this minimum therefore gives $$\label{eq:entropy-dual-minimum}
 \begin{aligned}
 \min_{r\ge0}\bigl(\mathcal F_t(1,r)+hr\bigr)
 &=t-D(D(t)+h)\\
 &=\mathcal L(t,h):=
   \log_2\!\left(1+(2^t-1)(1-2^{-h})\right).
 \end{aligned}$$ For $t=0$, both the minimum and $\mathcal L(0,h)$ are zero, so the equality between the first and last expressions holds there as well; the middle expression is used only for $t>0$.

<a id="eq:budget-multipliers"></a>
For a given $w$, it is enough to find $\lambda,\mu\ge0$ such that $$\label{eq:budget-multipliers}
 \begin{aligned}
 2\lambda+\mu+\mathcal L(w,\mu)&\ge\beta,\\
 3\lambda+2\mu+
   \mathcal L(2w,\beta+\lambda+2\mu)&\ge6-\beta,\\
 (\lambda+\mu)/2&\le c^*(w).
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, homogeneity and Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-dual-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-dual-minimum">[eq:entropy-dual-minimum]</a> imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal F_w(g,v_0)+\mu v_0&\ge g\,\mathcal L(w,\mu),\\
 \mathcal F_{2w}(a_0,u)+(\beta+\lambda+2\mu)u
   &\ge a_0\,\mathcal L(2w,\beta+\lambda+2\mu).
 \end{aligned}$$ These remain true when $g=0$ or $a_0=0$. Using the first two inequalities in Equation <a href="#eq:budget-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-multipliers">[eq:budget-multipliers]</a> now gives $$\mathcal F_w(g,v_0)+\mathcal F_{2w}(a_0,u)
 +\lambda(2g+3a_0+u)+\mu(g+v_0+2a_0+2u)\ge I.$$ The two budgets and the third inequality in Equation <a href="#eq:budget-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-multipliers">[eq:budget-multipliers]</a> yield Equation <a href="#eq:budget-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-dual">[eq:budget-dual]</a>.

<a id="eq:L-linear-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:D-uniform-bound"></a>
**Exact verification.** We verify Equation <a href="#eq:budget-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-multipliers">[eq:budget-multipliers]</a> with three explicit choices. The function $D$ is decreasing, and, for $h>0$, $$\label{eq:L-linear-bound}
 \mathcal L(t,h)\ge t-D(h).$$ This follows by dropping the positive term $2^{-h}$ from $2^{\mathcal L(t,h)}=2^t(1-2^{-h})+2^{-h}$. Only one numerical estimate for $D$ is needed: $$\label{eq:D-uniform-bound}
 D(21/10)<2/5.$$ Indeed, if $v=2^{-1/10}$, then $v<14/15$ because $15^{10}<2\cdot14^{10}$. Thus $$2^{-21/10}+2^{-2/5}
 =\frac v4+v^4
 <\frac7{30}+\left(\frac{14}{15}\right)^4
 =\frac{100457}{101250}<1,$$ which is equivalent to Equation <a href="#eq:D-uniform-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:D-uniform-bound">[eq:D-uniform-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:dual-support-lines"></a>
We will use the support bounds $$\label{eq:dual-support-lines}
 2c^*(w)\ge
 \max\left\{2-w,\ \frac32-\frac w2,\
             \frac{23}{24}-\frac w6,\ \frac2{w+2}\right\}.$$ The first three follow by testing $q=1/2,1/4,1/12$ in the definition of $c^*$. The first two values are $c(1/2)=1$ and $c(1/4)=3/4$. At $q=1/12$, the argument of $z$ is $1/5$, the square root in its definition is $3/5$, and $c(1/12)=1-(5/6)(5/8)=23/48$. For the last bound, when $0\le q\le1/4$, put $$r=\frac{2\sqrt{q(1-3q)}}{1-2q}.$$ Then $$r^2-4q=\frac{4q^2(1-4q)}{(1-2q)^2}\ge0.$$ The expression $(r+2q)/(1+r)$ increases in $r$, so $$c(q)=\frac{r+2q}{1+r}
 \ge\frac{2\sqrt q+2q}{1+2\sqrt q}
 \ge2\sqrt q-2q.$$ The difference in the last inequality is $4q\sqrt q/(1+2\sqrt q)$. Testing $q=(w+2)^{-2}\le1/4$ now gives $$2c^*(w)\ge2\bigl(c(q)-wq\bigr)
 \ge2\left(\frac2{w+2}-\frac{w+2}{(w+2)^2}\right)
 =\frac2{w+2},$$ including at $w=0$.

<a id="eq:L-quarter-bound"></a>
One global estimate handles the middle range: $$\label{eq:L-quarter-bound}
 \mathcal L(w,1/4)>\frac{w-1}{2}\qquad(w\ge0).$$ To prove it, let $b=2^{-1/4}$. The integer inequality $20^4<2\cdot17^4$ gives $1/2<b<17/20$. Since $b(1-b)$ decreases for $b\ge1/2$, $$b(1-b)>\frac{17}{20}\frac3{20}
 =\frac{51}{400}>\frac18.$$ Consequently the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives $$2^{\mathcal L(w,1/4)}=b+(1-b)2^w
 \ge2\sqrt{b(1-b)2^w}>2^{(w-1)/2},$$ which proves Equation <a href="#eq:L-quarter-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-quarter-bound">[eq:L-quarter-bound]</a>.

We use the following choices, writing $h=\beta+\lambda+2\mu$: $$(\lambda,\mu)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\left(\max\left\{2-w,\frac32-\frac w2\right\},0\right),
     &\displaystyle 0\le w\le\frac85,\\[6pt]
 \displaystyle\left(\frac{17}{24}-\frac w6,\frac14\right),
     &\displaystyle \frac85\le w\le3,\\[6pt]
 (0,3\,2^{-w}),&w\ge3.
 \end{cases}$$ Either choice works at a shared endpoint.

For $0\le w\le8/5$, put $S=\max\{2-w,3/2-w/2\}$, so $\lambda=S$ and $\mu=0$. Then $S\ge7/10$, so $h\ge21/10$ and $$2\lambda+\mathcal L(w,0)=2S\ge7/5.$$ By Equations <a href="#eq:L-linear-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-linear-bound">[eq:L-linear-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:D-uniform-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:D-uniform-bound">[eq:D-uniform-bound]</a>, the second multiplier expression exceeds $$3S+2w-\frac25
 =\max\left\{\frac{28}{5}-w,\frac{41}{10}+\frac w2\right\}
 \ge\frac{23}{5}.$$ For the last inequality, use the first term when $w\le1$ and the second when $w\ge1$. The choices are nonnegative, and the third multiplier inequality, in the equivalent form $\lambda+\mu\le2c^*(w)$, follows directly from Equation <a href="#eq:dual-support-lines" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-support-lines">[eq:dual-support-lines]</a>.

For $8/5\le w\le3$, take $\lambda=17/24-w/6$ and $\mu=1/4$. These are nonnegative, and $$h\ge\frac75+\frac5{24}+\frac12
 =\frac{253}{120}>\frac{21}{10}.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:L-quarter-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-quarter-bound">[eq:L-quarter-bound]</a>, the first multiplier expression exceeds $$2\lambda+\mu+\frac{w-1}{2}
 =\frac76+\frac w6
 \ge\frac{43}{30}>\frac75.$$ By Equations <a href="#eq:L-linear-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-linear-bound">[eq:L-linear-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:D-uniform-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:D-uniform-bound">[eq:D-uniform-bound]</a>, the second expression exceeds $$3\lambda+2\mu+2w-\frac25
 =\frac{89}{40}+\frac32w
 \ge\frac{37}{8}>\frac{23}{5}.$$ Finally, $\lambda+\mu=23/24-w/6$, so Equation <a href="#eq:dual-support-lines" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-support-lines">[eq:dual-support-lines]</a> gives the third inequality.

For $w\ge3$, take $\lambda=0$ and $\mu=3\,2^{-w}$, which are nonnegative. The elementary bound $$\log2=2\int_0^{1/3}\frac{dt}{1-t^2}>\frac23$$ and $2^\mu-1\ge\mu\log2$ give $$2^{\mu+\mathcal L(w,\mu)}
 =1+2^w(2^\mu-1)
 \ge1+3\log2>3>2^{7/5}.$$ The last inequality follows from $3^5>2^7$. This proves the first multiplier inequality. Here $h\ge\beta>1$, so $D(h)<D(1)=1$. Equation <a href="#eq:L-linear-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-linear-bound">[eq:L-linear-bound]</a> therefore shows that the second expression is greater than $$2w-1\ge5>\frac{23}{5}.$$ For the third inequality, $3\,2^{-3}=3/8<2/5$, and $2^w/(w+2)$ increases for $w\ge3$, since its logarithmic derivative is $$\log2-\frac1{w+2}>\frac23-\frac15>0.$$ It follows that $\mu\le2/(w+2)\le2c^*(w)$ by Equation <a href="#eq:dual-support-lines" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-support-lines">[eq:dual-support-lines]</a>. This verifies Equation <a href="#eq:budget-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-multipliers">[eq:budget-multipliers]</a> for every $w\ge0$, and hence proves Equation <a href="#eq:budget-primal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-primal">[eq:budget-primal]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:minor-paths"></a>
### Paths of principal minors

We prove the potential moment bound by following paths of nonsingular principal minors. A tail estimate first controls the layers of large height. On a short terminal window, either the potential is small at some node, or a definite decrease of a second statistic forces many steps that each have small conditional probability.

<a id="eq:path-tail-difference"></a>
For a nonsingular matrix $B$ and an integer $s\ge0$, define $$W_s(B)=\sum_{p,e}(k_B(p,e)-s)_+\nu_p,
 \qquad x_+=\max\{x,0\}.$$ In the layer diagram of Figure <a href="#fig:layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:layers">1</a>, the factor $(k_B(p,e)-s)_+$ counts the boxes in row $e$ beyond the first $s$ columns; the sum weights them by $\nu_p$. When a tail occurs together with a nonsingularity requirement, set $W_s(B)=0$ on singular matrices. The order bound in Equation <a href="#eq:group-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:group-order">[eq:group-order]</a> gives $0\le W_s(B)\le1/2$. For $s\ge1$, we also have $$\label{eq:path-tail-difference}
 W_{s-1}(B)-W_s(B)=\sum_{\substack{p,e\\ k_B(p,e)\ge s}}\nu_p.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-recursion"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-recursion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:path-tail-recursion"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Tail recursion). *Let $C\to B$ be a transition of width $h\in\{1,2\}$ between nonsingular matrices. In the $i$-th successive quotient of $G_C$, write $k_{i-1}(p,e)$ for the height before that quotient and $\mathcal R_i$ for its retained layers. For every integer $s\ge h$, $$\label{eq:path-tail-recursion}
 W_s(B)\le W_{s+h}(C)
   +2\sum_{i=1}^{h}
       \sum_{\substack{(p,e)\in\mathcal R_i\\
                       k_{i-1}(p,e)\ge s+1-h}}\nu_p.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-recursion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-recursion</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a prime $p$, and put $F_D(p,t)=\sum_{e=1}^{t}k_D(p,e)$ for $D=C,B$. Take $t$ to be the largest $e$ for which $k_B(p,e)>s$, or take $t=0$ when there is no such $e$. Then $$\sum_{e\ge1}(k_B(p,e)-s)_+=F_B(p,t)-st.$$ For $e\le t$, the height in $G_B$ is at least $s+1$. The final successive quotient of $G_C$ is also obtained from $G_B$ by $h$ one-element quotients. The interlacing of heights under each such quotient therefore gives $$k_{i-1}(p,e)\ge k_B(p,e)-h\ge s+1-h\ge1
 \qquad (e\le t,\ 1\le i\le h).$$ Let $u_i(p,t)$ be the total drop in the first $t$ layers at the $i$-th quotient and put $U_p(t)=\sum_i u_i(p,t)$. Each layer in this range has positive starting height, and each of its drops is zero or one. Thus $$ht-U_p(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{h}
       \#\{e\le t:(p,e)\in\mathcal R_i\}.$$ All the layers on the right have the height lower bound just displayed. Lemma <a href="#lem:layer-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:layer-inequality">30</a> now yields $$\begin{split}
 F_B(p,t)-st
 &\le F_C(p,t)-(s+h)t+2\bigl(ht-U_p(t)\bigr)\\
 &\le \sum_{e\ge1}(k_C(p,e)-s-h)_+
       +2\sum_{i=1}^{h}
          \#\{e:(p,e)\in\mathcal R_i,\ k_{i-1}(p,e)\ge s+1-h\}.
 \end{split}$$ Multiplying by $\nu_p$ and summing over $p$ proves the claim. ◻

</details>

Recall the scales $R=\lceil\ell^2\rceil$ and $\sigma=R^{-6}$ used in Proposition <a href="#prop:retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:retention-cost">35</a>. The next estimate applies without a typicality hypothesis.

<a id="stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost"></a>
<div id="lem:heavy-retention-cost" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38** (Cost of heavy retained layers). *Let $\mathcal F$ denote exposed information which fixes a nonsingular binary matrix $C$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ and a quotient map $\pi:\mathbb Z^m\twoheadrightarrow H$ with $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\ker\pi$. Suppose that, conditionally on $\mathcal F$, a tested column $v$ is uniform on $\{0,1\}^m$. If $\mathcal R$ is the set of layers retained on quotienting $H$ by $\pi(v)$, put $$Q=\sum_{\substack{(p,e)\in\mathcal R\\ k_H(p,e)\ge R-1}}
           k_H(p,e)\nu_p.$$ Fix $0<\eta<1$ and set $q_0=\eta/(100\log N)$. Uniformly for $q_0\le q\le1/2$, and for all sufficiently large $N$ depending on $\eta$, <a id="eq:path-heavy-retention"></a>$$\label{eq:path-heavy-retention}
 \Pr\{Q\in[q,2q)\mid\mathcal F\}\le 2^{-3qN/5}.$$ The exposed information may include the preceding column of a width-two transition, provided the tested column remains conditionally uniform.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost</summary>

*Proof.* Conditioning on $\mathcal F$ fixes the quotient map and $H$; it leaves $v$ uniform on $\{0,1\}^m$. For a fixed mask $E$ of layers required to be retained, Lemma <a href="#lem:retention-lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:retention-lattice">29</a> supplies the necessary condition $$v\in K_E,\qquad
 C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K_E\subseteq\mathbb Z^m,\qquad
 [\mathbb Z^m:K_E]=N^{q(E)N},\qquad
 q(E)=\sum_{(p,e)\in E}k_H(p,e)\nu_p.$$ We first show that this condition costs at least $2q(E)N/3$ bits of probability.

Write $g_1,\ldots,g_m$ for the images of the coordinate vectors in $\mathbb Z^m/K_E$. Choose a maximal subset $g_{i_1},\ldots,g_{i_j}$ that is dissociated, meaning that all of its subset sums are distinct. After the other bits of $v$ are fixed, at most one choice of the $j$ selected bits can give any prescribed group element. Hence every atom of the image of $v$, in particular the zero atom, has probability at most $2^{-j}$.

<a id="eq:path-fixed-mask"></a>
Maximality also implies that every $g_a$ is a combination of the selected elements with coefficients in $\{0,1,-1\}$: otherwise adjoining $g_a$ would preserve distinctness of subset sums. Choose such representations, using the identity representations at the selected indices, and arrange their coefficients as the columns of a matrix $M\in\mathbb Z^{j\times m}$. This matrix has rank $j$. The selected elements generate $\mathbb Z^m/K_E$, so they define a surjection $\theta:\mathbb Z^j\to\mathbb Z^m/K_E$. Every column of $MC$ belongs to $\ker\theta$, because the columns of $C$ belong to $K_E$. Since $C$ is nonsingular, $MC$ still has rank $j$. Choose $j$ independent columns of $MC$. Each of their entries has absolute value at most $m\le N$. Their lattice is contained in $\ker\theta$, and Hadamard’s inequality gives $$|\mathbb Z^m/K_E|=[\mathbb Z^j:\ker\theta]
 \le (N\sqrt j)^j\le N^{3j/2}.$$ For $j=0$, the group is trivial and the same conclusion has its usual empty-product meaning. It follows that $$\label{eq:path-fixed-mask}
 \Pr\{v\in K_E\mid\mathcal F\}\le2^{-j}\le2^{-2q(E)N/3}.$$

We next bound the number of possible masks consisting of all retained layers of height at least $R-1$, when their value of $Q$ lies in $[q,2q)$. At a fixed prime the retained layers on each plateau form a prefix. Let $d_p$ be the number of plateaus of $H$ at $p$. Their distinct positive heights imply $$d_p^2\le 2\sum_e k_H(p,e).$$ Moreover, $\sum_{p,e}k_H(p,e)\le\log_2|H|=O(N\log N)$. There are at most $N^{1/2}$ primes $p\le N^{1/2}$, so Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the number of their plateaus by $$\sum_{p\le N^{1/2}}d_p
   =O\bigl(N^{3/4}(\log N)^{1/2}\bigr).$$ Each prefix length has $O(N\log N)$ possible values and costs $O(\log N)$ bits. The full cost for these primes is consequently $$O\bigl(N^{3/4}(\log N)^{3/2}\bigr)
     =o(N/\log N)=o_\eta(qN).$$ Here the final estimate is uniform for $q\ge q_0$.

For $p>N^{1/2}$, each eligible layer has, for large $N$, $$k_H(p,e)\nu_p\ge\frac{R-1}{2N}\ge\frac{R}{3N}.$$ The total height times weight in $H$ is at most $1/2$, so there are at most $3N/(2R)$ eligible layers in this range. A mask with $Q<2q$ selects at most $6qN/R$ of them. The usual binomial estimate, using the bound $2^a$ for all subsets when the permitted number is a fixed fraction of the $a$ available layers, gives a logarithmic count of at most $$O\left(\frac{qN}{R}\bigl(1+\log(1/q)\bigr)\right)=o_\eta(qN).$$ Indeed $qN/R\ge\eta N/(100R\log N)\to\infty$, so integer rounding does not affect this estimate, and $$1+\log(1/q)\le 1+\log(100\log N/\eta)=O_\eta(\ell),
 \qquad R\asymp\ell^2.$$ Combining the two prime ranges, the mask entropy is $o_\eta(qN)$ uniformly in the indicated interval. For every mask being counted, $q(E)=Q\ge q$. The union bound with Equation <a href="#eq:path-fixed-mask" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-fixed-mask">[eq:path-fixed-mask]</a> is therefore $$\Pr\{Q\in[q,2q)\mid\mathcal F\}
 \le 2^{-(2/3-o_\eta(1))qN}\le2^{-3qN/5}.$$ The conditioning argument uses only that $\mathcal F$ fixes $C,\pi$ and leaves $v$ uniform, so it also applies when $\mathcal F$ includes a preceding column. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost</summary>

<a id="eq:path-scales"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:path-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:path-potential">8</a>.* Fix $0<\epsilon<1/10$, and put $$\epsilon'=\epsilon/20,\qquad
 J_*=2\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil,\qquad
 L_*=\lceil R^{12}\rceil.$$ Here $J_*$ is the length of the long path, and $L_*$ is the length of its terminal window. The following comparisons will be used throughout: $$\label{eq:path-scales}
 \begin{gathered}
 2J_*+1<N^{7/10},\qquad
 L_*\asymp\ell^{24}=o(N^{3/5}),\qquad
 J_*-L_*>\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil,\\
 L_*\ell^{-60}=O(\ell^{-36})=o(1),\qquad
 \frac{L_*\sigma}{R^3}\asymp R^3\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 \frac{N\sigma}{\log R}=\frac{N}{R^6\log R}\longrightarrow\infty.
 \end{gathered}$$ They hold for all sufficiently large $N$, since $R\asymp\ell^2$ and every fixed power of $\ell$ is $o(N^a)$ for every fixed $a>0$. The first line keeps every node in $\mathcal I_N$ and leaves enough preceding steps to drive the tail threshold past $N^{3/5}$. The second line keeps the potential-step error small over the terminal window, makes the accumulated costly-step exponent diverge, and makes that cost dominate the entropy of choosing the costly positions.

<a id="eq:path-typical-exception"></a>
For $m\in\mathcal I_N$, let $\mathcal T_m$ be the set of nonsingular matrices satisfying the estimates in Propositions <a href="#prop:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-corank">20</a> and <a href="#prop:retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:retention-cost">35</a>, and those in Propositions <a href="#prop:admissible-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:admissible-potential">7</a> and <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a> at precision $A=60$, including both widths in the latter proposition. These finitely many properties can be chosen so that, for some $f(N)\to\infty$, $$\Pr\{\det\mathbf B_m\ne0,\ \mathbf B_m\notin\mathcal T_m\}
 \le2^{-Nf(N)}
 \qquad(m\in\mathcal I_N).$$ Write $m_*=N-1$. Consider all principal submatrices of $\mathbf B_{m_*}$ obtained by deleting at most $2J_*$ indices. Their sizes lie in $\mathcal I_N$ by Equation <a href="#eq:path-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-scales">[eq:path-scales]</a>, and their number is at most $$\sum_{d\le2J_*}\binom{m_*}{d}
 \le2^{O(N^{3/5}\log N)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ Let $\mathcal E_N$ be the event that any nonsingular one of these submatrices fails its typical properties. Each fixed submatrix is a binary matrix, so the union bound gives $$\label{eq:path-typical-exception}
 \Pr(\mathcal E_N)
 \le 2^{-N(f(N)-O(N^{-2/5}\log N))}=2^{-\omega(N)}.$$ Since $2^{N\Psi}\le2^N$, its contribution to the moment is at most $$2^N\Pr(\mathcal E_N)
 \le2^{-N(f(N)-1-O(N^{-2/5}\log N))}=2^{-\omega(N)}.$$

Starting from a nonsingular realization on $[m_*]$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:gated-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gated-deletion">26</a> backwards for exactly $J_*$ steps. It gives a path of nonsingular principal minors, each deletion having width one or two, and every deletion of width two satisfying the gate when read as a forward transition. At most $2J_*$ indices are deleted, so all these operations stay in the prescribed size range. On $\mathcal E_N^c$, all of their nodes also belong to their respective sets $\mathcal T_m$.

<a id="eq:path-cover"></a>
For clarity, the path just obtained is chosen backwards as a function of the realized terminal matrix. We use it only to cover the terminal event by path events. Let $\Pi_N$ be the collection of all *fixed* index paths $$I_0\subset I_1\subset\cdots\subset I_{J_*}=[m_*],
 \qquad h_t=|I_t\setminus I_{t-1}|\in\{1,2\}.$$ An ordering of the two indices in a pair is fixed once for each path. At each backward step there are at most $2N^2$ choices, whence $$|\Pi_N|\le(2N^2)^{J_*}
       =2^{O(N^{3/5}\log N)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ For a fixed $\pi\in\Pi_N$, put $B_t=\mathbf B_{m_*}[I_t]$. Let $\Gamma_t$ be the event $\mathcal G_{h_t}$ from Equation <a href="#eq:extension-gate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extension-gate">[eq:extension-gate]</a> for step $t$. Define $$\mathcal A_\pi
   =\bigcap_{t=0}^{J_*}\{B_t\in\mathcal T_{|I_t|}\}
      \ \cap\ \bigcap_{t=1}^{J_*}\Gamma_t.$$ The backward construction shows that every nonsingular terminal matrix outside $\mathcal E_N$ lies in at least one $\mathcal A_\pi$. Consequently $$\label{eq:path-cover}
 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\det\mathbf B_{m_*}\ne0\}}\,
                 2^{N\Psi(\mathbf B_{m_*})}\right]
 \le2^N\Pr(\mathcal E_N)
       +\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_N}
          \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_\pi}2^{N\Psi(B_{J_*})}\right].$$ We now bound a summand for a fixed $\pi$. Expose the entries of $B_0$ first and then expose each transition forwards. Conditional on the entries already exposed, its cross columns are independent uniform bit vectors on the starting indices, and its new symmetric block consists of independent bits. Within a pair the second cross column is still uniform after the first is exposed. We sum over fixed paths before making any of these exposures; we never condition this forward process on the backward choice of a path. The future nonsingularity, typicality, and gate requirements are imposed only through indicators.

Call the last $L_*$ steps and their $L_*+1$ nodes the terminal window. We will prove the three weighted bounds in Table <a href="#tab:path-contributions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:path-contributions">1</a>.

<div id="tab:path-contributions">

| Covered event                                               | Terminal weighted contribution         |
|:------------------------------------------------------------|:---------------------------------------|
| Some window node has $W_R>\epsilon'$                        | $2^{N-\Omega_\epsilon(NR)}$            |
| Some window node has $\Psi\le\epsilon$                      | $(L_*+1)2^{N(\epsilon+L_*\ell^{-60})}$ |
| Every window node has $W_R\le\epsilon'$ and $\Psi>\epsilon$ | $2^{N-\Omega_\epsilon(NR^3)}$          |

Bounds to prove for a cover of the terminal weighted contribution of one fixed path. Each row includes the indicator of $\mathcal A_\pi$, the indicator of its displayed event, and the weight $2^{N\Psi(B_{J_*})}$. The first two events may overlap.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Large tails in the window.

<a id="eq:path-heavy-moment"></a>
At each cross column of the fixed path, form $Q$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:heavy-retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heavy-retention-cost">38</a> using the quotient immediately before that column. Put $$q_{\min}=\frac{\epsilon'}{100\log N},\qquad
 \widetilde Q=
 \begin{cases}
 Q,&Q\ge q_{\min},\\
 0,&Q<q_{\min}.
 \end{cases}$$ For an unconditional exposure on which the starting matrix $C$ of a step is singular, define $\widetilde Q=0$ for its cross columns. This convention permits estimates on the entire probability space. For a nonsingular start, $Q\le1/2$ by the group order bound. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:heavy-retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heavy-retention-cost">38</a> with $\eta=\epsilon'$ to the dyadic values $q_j=2^j q_{\min}$ up to $1/2$. Conditional on everything exposed before a cross column, $$\begin{split}
 \mathbb E\!\left[2^{N\widetilde Q/4}\mid
                    \hbox{previous exposures}\right]
 &\le1+\sum_{j:q_j\le1/2}
          2^{Nq_j/2}\,2^{-3Nq_j/5}\\
 &\le1+\sum_{j:q_j\le1/2}2^{-Nq_j/10}
 \le1+\delta_N,\qquad
 \delta_N=2^{-\epsilon'N/(2000\log N)}.
 \end{split}$$ The last inequality holds for large $N$, since $Nq_{\min}\to\infty$; the dyadic sum is at most twice its first term and is then bounded by the displayed $\delta_N$. It holds also for a singular start by the convention above. There are $d_\pi=\sum_t h_t\le2J_*=O(N^{3/5})$ cross columns. Successive conditioning, including between the two columns of a pair, therefore gives the multiplicative bound $$\label{eq:path-heavy-moment}
 \mathbb E\,2^{(N/4)\sum_{a=1}^{d_\pi}\widetilde Q_a}
 \le(1+\delta_N)^{d_\pi}
 \le\exp\!\bigl(O(N^{3/5}\delta_N)\bigr)=1+o(1).$$ In particular the accumulated error in its logarithm is $O(N^{3/5}2^{-\epsilon'N/(2000\log N)})=o(1)$.

On $\mathcal A_\pi$, suppose $W_R(B_t)>\epsilon'$ at a node $J_*-L_*\le t\le J_*$. Starting at this node, iterate Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-recursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-recursion">37</a> backwards, increasing $s$ by the width at every step. Stop as soon as $s>N^{3/5}$. This takes at most $\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ steps, so the available preceding path is long enough by Equation <a href="#eq:path-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-scales">[eq:path-scales]</a>. Every invocation has $s\ge R\ge2$, as required. At the last node the tail is zero: Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-corank">20</a> gives $$k_B(p,e)\le k_B(p,1)=\mathop{\mathrm{corank}}_{\mathbb F_p}(B\bmod p)\le N^{3/5}$$ at every typical nonsingular node and every prime $p$.

<a id="eq:path-large-tail"></a>
Number the backwards steps from the chosen node by $j=1,2,\ldots,r$. Before step $j$, the current value of $s$ is $R$ plus the widths of its $j-1$ predecessors. If the current width is $h\le2$, each charged layer in Equation <a href="#eq:path-tail-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-tail-recursion">[eq:path-tail-recursion]</a> thus has height at least $$s+1-h\ge R+j-2.$$ The charge from one quotient at this step is bounded by $2Q/(R+j-2)$, because $Q$ includes height times weight for all retained layers of height at least $R-1$. There are at most two quotients per step. The terms with $Q<q_{\min}$ contribute at most $$4q_{\min}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac1{R+j-2}
 \le4q_{\min}(1+\log N)\le\epsilon'/2$$ for large $N$. Here $r\le\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ and the last comparison follows from the definition of $q_{\min}$. The remaining charges must therefore exceed $\epsilon'/2$. Since every denominator is at least $R-1$, this implies $$\sum_{a=1}^{d_\pi}\widetilde Q_a
       \ge\frac{\epsilon'(R-1)}4.$$ The same condition on the sum over the whole path is necessary if any node of the window has $W_R>\epsilon'$. By Markov’s inequality and Equation <a href="#eq:path-heavy-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-heavy-moment">[eq:path-heavy-moment]</a>, $$\label{eq:path-large-tail}
 \begin{split}
 &\Pr\!\left(\mathcal A_\pi,\
       \max_{J_*-L_*\le t\le J_*}W_R(B_t)>\epsilon'\right)\\
 &\qquad\le
 2^{-\epsilon'N(R-1)/16}\,
      \exp\!\bigl(O(N^{3/5}\delta_N)\bigr)
 =2^{-\Omega_\epsilon(NR)}=2^{-\omega(N)}.
 \end{split}$$ Multiplication by the terminal weight, which is at most $2^N$, still gives $2^{-\omega(N)}$, because $R\to\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### A small potential in the window.

<a id="eq:path-low-window"></a>
Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-step">27</a> at precision $60$ states, for either width and for a starting matrix $C\in\mathcal T_m$, $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\det B\ne0\}}\mathbf 1_{\Gamma}\,
                   2^{N\Psi(B)}\mid C\right]
       \le2^{N(\Psi(C)+\ell^{-60})},$$ where $\Gamma$ is the gate for width two and the certain event for width one. Adding a typicality indicator at the endpoint only decreases the left side. Successive conditioning from a node $t$ to the terminal node consequently bounds the conditional weighted expectation with all the intervening requirements by $$2^{N\Psi(B_t)}\,2^{N(J_*-t)\ell^{-60}}$$ whenever $B_t\in\mathcal T_{|I_t|}$. This is an application to the fixed forward exposure, with no conditioning on future requirements. For each $t$ in the window, on $\Psi(B_t)\le\epsilon$ it is at most $2^{N(\epsilon+L_*\ell^{-60})}$. Dropping all requirements before $t$ and summing over its $L_*+1$ possible values gives $$\label{eq:path-low-window}
 \begin{split}
 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_\pi}
       \mathbf 1_{\{\min_{J_*-L_*\le t\le J_*}\Psi(B_t)\le\epsilon\}}
                  2^{N\Psi(B_{J_*})}\right]
 &\le(L_*+1)\,2^{N(\epsilon+L_*\ell^{-60})}\\
 &=2^{N(\epsilon+O(\ell^{-36})+O(\log R/N))}.
 \end{split}$$ This records both the accumulated transition error and the cost of choosing the node.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### The remaining window event.

<a id="eq:path-window-two"></a>
<a id="eq:path-window-three"></a>
Consider the event $$\mathcal H_\pi=\mathcal A_\pi
  \cap\left\{\max_{J_*-L_*\le t\le J_*}W_R(B_t)\le\epsilon'\right\}
  \cap\left\{\min_{J_*-L_*\le t\le J_*}\Psi(B_t)>\epsilon\right\}.$$ At any node $B$ of this event, $\phi(k)\le2k$ and $\phi(0)=\phi(1)=0$ show that $\sum_{k_B\ge2}k_B\nu_p\ge\epsilon/2$. On the other hand, $$\sum_{\substack{p,e\\ k_B(p,e)>2R}}k_B(p,e)\nu_p
 \le2W_R(B)\le2\epsilon'.$$ Dividing the contribution from $2\le k_B\le2R$ by $2R$ gives $$\label{eq:path-window-two}
 \sum_{\substack{p,e\\k_B(p,e)\ge2}}\nu_p
 \ge\frac{\epsilon/2-2\epsilon'}{2R}
 \ge\frac{\epsilon}{10R}.$$ If in addition $\Psi(B)>1-1/R$, the contribution to $\Psi(B)$ from height two is at most $\beta/4=7/20$. Indeed, $2\sum_{k_B=2}\nu_p\le1/2$ by the order bound. Thus $$2\sum_{\substack{p,e\\k_B(p,e)\ge3}}k_B(p,e)\nu_p
  =\Psi(B)-\beta\sum_{\substack{p,e\\k_B(p,e)=2}}\nu_p
  >1-\frac1R-\frac{\beta}{4}.$$ For large $N$ the height times weight on the left after division by two is at least $1/4$. Since $\epsilon'<1/200$, deleting the layers above $2R$ and dividing by $2R$ yields $$\label{eq:path-window-three}
 \sum_{\substack{p,e\\k_B(p,e)\ge3}}\nu_p
 \ge\frac{1/4-2\epsilon'}{2R}\ge\frac1{20R}.$$

For each quotient at step $t$, let $V_{t,i}$ be the sum of the weights $\nu_p$ of all of its retained layers. On $\mathcal A_\pi$, call a step in the window costly if some $V_{t,i}\ge\sigma$, or if it has width two and its starting node $C=B_{t-1}$ satisfies $\Psi(C)\le1-1/R$. To price this description without conditioning on $\mathcal A_\pi$, define an exposure event $D_t$ as follows: the starting node belongs to its set $\mathcal T_m$, and either some $V_{t,i}\ge\sigma$, or the step has width two, $\Psi(C)\le1-1/R$, and its gate $\Gamma_t$ holds. Every costly step of a required path satisfies $D_t$.

<a id="eq:path-costly-step"></a>
<a id="eq:path-costly-set"></a>
Let $\mathcal F_{t-1}$ be the sigma-field generated by all entries exposed through $C$. Proposition <a href="#prop:retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:retention-cost">35</a>, applied to each column and conditioned on a preceding column when needed, gives probability at most $2^{-N\sigma/2}$ for each event $V_{t,i}\ge\sigma$. For the other alternative, the two cross columns are conditionally independent and the gate requires both to belong to $\mathcal S(C)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:admissible-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:admissible-potential">7</a> gives $$\begin{split}
 \Pr(\Gamma_t\mid\mathcal F_{t-1})
 &=\left(\frac{|\mathcal S(C)|}{2^m}\right)^2\\
 &\le2^{-2N(1-\Psi(C)-\ell^{-60}-(1-m/N))}.
 \end{split}$$ Here $1-m/N\le N^{-3/10}$. If $\Psi(C)\le1-1/R$, then $\ell^{-60}+N^{-3/10}=o(R^{-1})$ and $\sigma=R^{-6}=o(R^{-1})$, so for large $N$ this probability is at most $2^{-N/R}\le2^{-N\sigma/2}$. If the start is not in $\mathcal T_m$, the event $D_t$ is empty. The union bound therefore gives, uniformly in the revealed history, $$\label{eq:path-costly-step}
 \Pr(D_t\mid\mathcal F_{t-1})
 \le p_N,\qquad p_N=3\,2^{-N\sigma/2}.$$ In particular, for any fixed set $T$ of step positions, ordinary successive conditioning in the forward exposure gives $$\label{eq:path-costly-set}
 \Pr\left(\bigcap_{t\in T}D_t\right)\le p_N^{|T|}.$$ This estimate imposes no conditioning on the other path requirements.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### Descent at noncostly steps.

To force many costly steps on $\mathcal H_\pi$, we combine two tails. At width one, the smaller $R^{-2}W_1$ term uses the mass of layers of height at least two. At width two, $W_2$ uses the stronger gap between $W_2$ and $W_4$, while the small coefficient prevents the $W_1$ term from offsetting that gap. Define $$Z(B)=W_2(B)+R^{-2}W_1(B).$$ The order bound implies $0\le Z(B)\le(1+R^{-2})/2\le1$. At a noncostly step all its $V_{t,i}$ are less than $\sigma$. For a step of width one, Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-recursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-recursion">37</a> gives $$W_2(B)\le W_3(C)+2\sigma\le W_2(C)+2\sigma,
 \qquad W_1(B)\le W_2(C)+2\sigma.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:path-tail-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-tail-difference">[eq:path-tail-difference]</a> and <a href="#eq:path-window-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-window-two">[eq:path-window-two]</a> then show $$Z(B)-Z(C)
 \le2\sigma(1+R^{-2})
       -R^{-2}\bigl(W_1(C)-W_2(C)\bigr)
 \le2\sigma(1+R^{-2})-\frac{\epsilon}{10R^3}
 \le-\frac{\epsilon}{20R^3}$$ for large $N$, since $\sigma=R^{-6}=o_\epsilon(R^{-3})$. At a noncostly step of width two, its starting potential is $\Psi(C)>1-1/R$. Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-recursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-recursion">37</a> gives $$W_2(B)\le W_4(C)+4\sigma.$$ Also $$W_2(C)-W_4(C)
 =\sum_{\substack{p,e\\k_C(p,e)\ge3}}
        \min\{k_C(p,e)-2,2\}\nu_p
 \ge\frac1{20R}$$ by Equation <a href="#eq:path-window-three" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-window-three">[eq:path-window-three]</a>. Using $W_1(B)\le1/2$, we obtain $$Z(B)-Z(C)
 \le-\frac1{20R}+4\sigma+\frac1{2R^2}
 \le-\frac1{40R}\le-\frac{\epsilon}{20R^3}$$ for all sufficiently large $N$. The middle comparison uses $4R^{-6}+(2R^2)^{-1}=o(R^{-1})$. Thus every noncostly step decreases $Z$ by at least $$\delta=\frac{\epsilon}{20R^3}.$$

<a id="eq:path-high-window"></a>
Let $n_{\mathrm{cost}}$ be the number of costly steps in this window. A costly step can increase $Z$ by at most one. Telescoping the $L_*$ changes and using $0\le Z\le1$ gives $$-1\le Z(B_{J_*})-Z(B_{J_*-L_*})
       \le n_{\mathrm{cost}}-(L_*-n_{\mathrm{cost}})\delta,
 \qquad
 n_{\mathrm{cost}}\ge\frac{L_*\delta-1}{1+\delta}.$$ Now $\delta\to0$ and $L_*\delta\asymp_\epsilon R^9\to\infty$. Consequently, for large $N$, $$n_{\mathrm{cost}}\ge \frac{L_*\epsilon}{100R^3}.$$ Set $t_*=\lceil L_*\epsilon/(100R^3)\rceil$. The event $\mathcal H_\pi$ therefore implies $D_t$ at at least $t_*$ positions in the window. Equations <a href="#eq:path-costly-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-costly-step">[eq:path-costly-step]</a> and <a href="#eq:path-costly-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-costly-set">[eq:path-costly-set]</a> yield $$\Pr(\mathcal H_\pi)\le\binom{L_*}{t_*}p_N^{t_*}.$$ The entropy of choosing these positions satisfies $$\log_2\binom{L_*}{t_*}
 \le t_*\log_2(eL_*/t_*)=O_\epsilon(t_*\log R).$$ Together with the factor $3^{t_*}$, this is $o(N\sigma t_*)$, because $N\sigma/\log R\to\infty$. In particular, for all sufficiently large $N$, $$\label{eq:path-high-window}
 \Pr(\mathcal H_\pi)
 \le2^{-N\sigma t_*/3}
 \le2^{-\epsilon N L_*\sigma/(300R^3)}
 =2^{-\Omega_\epsilon(NR^3)}
 =2^{-\omega(N)}.$$ This bound uses $L_*\sigma/R^3\asymp R^3\to\infty$. The corresponding weighted expectation is at most $2^{N-\Omega_\epsilon(NR^3)}=2^{-\omega(N)}$.

Equations <a href="#eq:path-large-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-large-tail">[eq:path-large-tail]</a>, <a href="#eq:path-low-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-low-window">[eq:path-low-window]</a>, and <a href="#eq:path-high-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-high-window">[eq:path-high-window]</a> prove the three weighted bounds in Table <a href="#tab:path-contributions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:path-contributions">1</a>. Its row events cover all outcomes in $\mathcal A_\pi$, so their sum gives, uniformly over all fixed index paths, $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_\pi}2^{N\Psi(B_{J_*})}\right]
 \le2^{N(\epsilon+o_\epsilon(1))}.$$ Finally use Equation <a href="#eq:path-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-cover">[eq:path-cover]</a>. The index-path sum adds $O(N^{3/5}\log N)=o(N)$ to the logarithm, and Equation <a href="#eq:path-typical-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-typical-exception">[eq:path-typical-exception]</a> gives a negligible exceptional contribution even after the weight $2^N$. Hence $$\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\det\mathbf B_{N-1}\ne0\}}\,
                 2^{N\Psi(\mathbf B_{N-1})}\right]
 \le2^{N(\epsilon+o_\epsilon(1))}.$$ For each fixed $\epsilon$ this estimate holds for all sufficiently large $N$. Letting $\epsilon$ tend to zero after $N$ proves Proposition <a href="#prop:path-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:path-potential">8</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heavy-retention-cost</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* By the implication established in Section <a href="#sec:framework" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:framework">2</a>, Propositions <a href="#prop:admissible-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:admissible-potential">7</a> and <a href="#prop:path-potential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:path-potential">8</a> give Equation <a href="#eq:column-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:column-target">[eq:column-target]</a>: $\mathbb E|\mathcal S(\mathbf B_{N-1})|\le2^{o(N)}$.

Lemmas <a href="#lem:corank-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:corank-reduction">5</a> and <a href="#lem:column-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:column-reduction">6</a> now give $$\Pr(\det\mathbf B_N=0)
 \le2^{o(N)}N2^{-N}\mathbb E|\mathcal S(\mathbf B_{N-1})|
       +2^{-\Omega(N^{6/5})}
 \le2^{-N+o(N)}.$$ A specified row of $\mathbf B_N$ is identically zero with probability $2^{-N}$, so the reverse bound follows from that event. Therefore $\Pr(\det\mathbf B_N=0)=2^{-N+o(N)}$. Finally Lemma <a href="#lem:sign-bit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sign-bit">2</a>, with $N=n-1$, yields $$\Pr(\det A_n=0)=2^{-(n-1)+o(n)}
                =2^{-n+o(n)}
                =\left(\frac12+o(1)\right)^n.$$ ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Layers for $G_{p} = ℤ/p^{5}ℤ ⊕ ℤ/p^{3}ℤ ⊕ ℤ/pℤ$ and its quotient by $v = (p^{2},p,1)$. The crossed boxes are the layer drops. Here $(u_{1},…,u_{5}) = (1,1,2,2,3)$, so the retained layers are $e = 2, 4$. On each plateau $e = 2, 3$ and $e = 4, 5$, retention is a prefix. The row lengths on the right are the heights of the quotient.",
      "figure_id": "fig:layers",
      "pdf_page": 33,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:layers",
          "exact_quote": "Layers for \\(G_p=\\Z/p^5\\Z\\oplus\\Z/p^3\\Z\\oplus\\Z/p\\Z\\)\nand its quotient by \\(v=(p^2,p,1)\\). The crossed boxes are the layer\ndrops. Here \\((u_1,\\ldots,u_5)=(1,1,2,2,3)\\), so the retained layers\nare \\(e=2,4\\). On each plateau \\(e=2,3\\) and \\(e",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.62cm,y=-0.62cm,\n                    every node/.style={font=\\small},\n                    line width=0.4pt]\n \\node at (1.5,-0.65) {\\(G_p\\)};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.25,-0.65) {\\(e\\)};\n \\foreach \\e/\\k in {1/3,2/2,3/2,4/1,5/1} {\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.25,{\\e-0.5}) {\\(\\e\\)};\n  \\foreach \\i in {1,...,\\k} {\n   \\draw ({\\i-1},{\\e-1}) rectangle (\\i,\\e);\n  }\n }\n \\foreach \\e/\\i in {1/3,3/2,5/1} {\n  \\draw[fill=black!12] ({\\i-1},{\\e-1}) rectangle (\\i,\\e);\n  \\node at ({\\i-0.5},{\\e-0.5}) {\\(\\times\\)};\n }\n \\node[anchor=west] at (3.25,1.5) {retained};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (3.25,3.5) {retained};\n \\draw[->] (6.5,2.5) -- (8.7,2.5);\n \\node at (7.6,1.85) {quotient by \\(v\\)};\n \\node at (11.5,-0.65) {\\(G_p/\\langle v\\rangle\\)};\n \\foreach \\e/\\k in {1/2,2/2,3/1,4/1} {\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (10.25,{\\e-0.5}) {\\(\\e\\)};\n  \\foreach \\i in {1,...,\\k} {\n   \\draw ({\\i+9.5},{\\e-1}) rectangle ({\\i+10.5},\\e);\n  }\n }\n \\node[anchor=east] at (10.25,4.5) {\\(5\\)};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (10.5,4.5) {height \\(0\\)};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A diagram with two grid/Young diagram representations side by side connected by a horizontal arrow pointing right labeled \"quotient by v\":\n- Left grid under column header $G_p$, with row indices $e$ from 1 to 5:\n  - Row 1: 3 square boxes, with an \"×\" inside the 3rd box.\n  - Row 2: 2 square boxes; labeled \"retained\" to the right of the row.\n  - Row 3: 2 square boxes, with an \"×\" inside the 2nd box.\n  - Row 4: 1 square box; labeled \"retained\" to the right of the row.\n  - Row 5: 1 square box, with an \"×\" inside the box.\n- Center: horizontal arrow pointing right labeled \"quotient by v\".\n- Right grid under column header $G_p/\\langle v\\rangle$, with row indices $e$ from 1 to 5:\n  - Row 1: 2 square boxes.\n  - Row 2: 2 square boxes.\n  - Row 3: 1 square box.\n  - Row 4: 1 square box.\n  - Row 5: 0 boxes, labeled \"height 0\" to the right of row index 5."
    }
  ],
